--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This is the title page for a work by Karl von Eckartshausen, a prominent 18th-century German philosopher and mystic. This page establishes the book's purpose: to guide the younger generation through the complex intellectual debates of the Enlightenment era.</meta>

### On
# Religion, Freethinking <note>original: "Freydenkerey." This term refers to the 18th-century intellectual movement that advocated for the application of reason to all areas of life, including the questioning of religious dogma.</note>
### and
# Enlightenment
### a
# Treatise
### on the writings of our times,
### dedicated to youth.

---

### By
## Karl von Eckartshausen.

Often, it is only in error that the cause

page 6

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal printer's ornament featuring symmetrical scrolled foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

therland, which binds the subject to his prince. Mutual mistrust creeps about in the dark and poisons the meadows where once joy breathed balsamic scents. The altars, which were once hallowed to the Deity, stand abandoned, and the roses lose their petals on the bushes from which wreaths were once woven for civic festivals. With tears in his eye, the friend of humanity <note>original: "Menschenfreund." This can also be translated as "philanthropist" in the 18th-century sense—someone who loves and wishes well for the whole human race.</note> sees the lamentable consequences of a terrible misunderstanding. Here the philosopher persecutes the honest, believing citizen; there the believing citizen persecutes the philosopher; here the cosmopolitan <term>cosmopolitan: "Kosmopolit" in this era referred to an "intellectual citizen of the world" who prioritized universal human concerns over local or national ones.</term> preaches universal love for humanity and forgets the love for the fatherland; there one wishes to love one's fellow citizen and insults universal humanity. What contradictions! What abomination! — And this in the age of Enlightenment! —

Is this called approaching the light, when men perform deeds of darkness? Is this called dispelling the night of prejudice, when fanatics of another kind thrust the burning torch of Enlightenment into the face of humanity — thrusting it so into the face that half-centuries will not be able to heal the burn scars dealt to it by learned stupidity <note>original: "gelehrte Dummheit." This refers to the arrogance of those who have much formal education but lack true wisdom or moral insight.</note>.

<summary>The author laments the social and religious divisions of the Enlightenment era, arguing that mutual mistrust and fanatical "reason" have replaced civic harmony and genuine faith.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, social discord, patriotism, cosmopolitanism, fanaticism, prejudice, learned stupidity, civic festivals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moral critique of the Enlightenment era from page 6. While the previous page lamented the social divisions between "philosophers" and "believers," this page focuses on the internal state of the individual, arguing that true Enlightenment must begin with self-reflection and personal virtue rather than the mere correction of others. The catchword "bei-" suggests the next page will continue the thought regarding "assistance" (beistand) or "examples" (beyspiele).</meta>

page 7

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal printer's ornament featuring a central floral or shell-like motif flanked by symmetrical scrolls and leafy patterns.</image-desc>

O humanity, how little you know your own heart! Dark clouds still cover your eyes; first illuminate the corners of your heart with the torch of Reason <note>original: "Vernunft." In Enlightenment philosophy, Reason was seen as the primary source of authority and legitimacy, often personified as a guiding light or torch.</note>, and banish harmful passions from your soul before you begin to spread light outside of yourselves. In our century, there is constant shouting about Enlightenment and Enlightenment, about prejudices and prejudices, yet people merely seat night upon the throne of darkness and replace old prejudices with new ones.

I am—I say it freely—not satisfied with the nature of our Enlightenment. Everyone seeks to improve others and forgets themselves, even though the only and best means of a true Enlightenment consists in this: to improve oneself, and through the enjoyment of the bliss of virtue <term>virtue: "Tugend" refers to moral excellence and the active practice of good, a central goal for Enlightenment thinkers.</term> and personal self-improvement, to show one’s fellow brother the value of piety <note>original: "Frömmigkeit." This term suggests a sincere, lived religious devotion rather than just formal church attendance.</note>. If every individual member is good, the entire body must necessarily become good. You, who wish to be an "Enlightener," let your own self-improvement be your first occupation. The language of your example has more power than your word, and the character of brotherly love in

<meta>catchword: bei-</meta>

<keywords>Enlightenment, Reason, self-improvement, virtue, piety, brotherly love, morality, social reform</keywords>

<page-num>8</page-num>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal ornament centered at the top of the page, featuring a central flower-like motif with symmetrical scrolling foliage on either side.</image-desc>

your actions proclaim the authenticity <note>original: "Aechtheit." This refers to the genuineness or sincerity of one's character, a major concern in 18th-century moral philosophy.</note> of your intentions. When people speak of improvements to humanity, while daily offending humanity; when philosophers wish to make other people happy, yet are too lazy to promote their own happiness, what is to be thought of their principles? That they are evil—no! I do not wish to say that, because I judge no person as evil; but I will say this: that even the good seed is choked by weeds when the sower casts it onto fields that the harrow <note>An agricultural tool used to break up clods of soil and remove weeds before sowing. Here, it serves as a metaphor for preparing the mind through self-discipline.</note> has not yet cleaned. Man is easily made a plaything by his own heart. Self-interest and self-love are our driving forces; they soon degenerate into evil if vigilance over ourselves does not impartially unfold our heart and make us strict judges of our own actions.

The calm of the passions is the first step toward wisdom; the sun’s image is not reflected in a stream that is murky; the spring must be pure; no storm may stir if the sun wishes to behold itself within it. Consider these principles, young men! And then judge yourselves. For the sake of humanity and your own best interests, I will

<page-num>9</page-num>

<image-desc>A decorative floral vignette ornament is centered at the top of the page.</image-desc>

I share my principles with you, you dear ones of my fatherland! I gathered them from nature—from experience, and from the knowledge of human nature <note>original: "Menschenkenntniß." In the 18th century, this was a specific field of study focused on understanding character, psychology, and social behavior.</note>. If you find that what I say to you is not in the nature of the matter, then call me a fool and laugh at me as a madman; but if I tell you things that are grounded in the knowledge of human nature, in pure philosophy, and in a noble intention for all of humanity, then I beg you: do not be deaf to my words!

Consider! He who speaks to you means well by you; he is himself still a contemporary <note>original: "Mitgespann." Literally a "harness-mate," suggesting someone traveling the same path or sharing the same burdens of the current generation.</note> of your own years; he is no grumpy old man whose gray hairs might make the matter suspicious to you. Youthful blood still flows in his veins; his heart still beats with the gentle feelings of human joys. He is not ashamed when a tear wets his cheeks over a sentimental <note>The author uses "empfindsam," a reference to the "Age of Sensibility" (Empfindsamkeit), where the ability to feel deep emotion was seen as a mark of a refined and moral character.</note> book, or when a tear rises to his eye during a moving tragedy <note>original: "Trauerspiel," referring to a tragic theatrical play.</note>. He lives with you in society; he is a citizen, a husband, and a father. He knows the feelings of friendship and love: his self-interest <note>original: "Eigennutz." Here, the author argues that his own success is naturally tied to the success and virtue of his fellow citizens.</note> is interwoven with your own; his principles are those which he would wish to instill in nature's dearest gifts—his children. And could you, despite all this, [doubt] his...

<summary>The author presents a personal appeal to his readers, framing himself not as a distant moral authority but as a fellow young citizen, husband, and father whose interests are tied to the common good. He emphasizes that his philosophy is rooted in shared human experience and the emotional sincerity of the "Age of Sensibility."</summary>

<keywords>Menschenkenntniß, philosophy, Sensibility, fatherland, citizenship, family, empathy, morality</keywords>

...your actions proclaim the authenticity of your intentions. When people speak of the improvement of humanity while offending humanity daily; when philosophers wish to make other people happy yet are too sluggish to promote their own happiness—what are we to make of their principles? That they are evil? No! I do not wish to say that, for I pass no evil judgment on any man. But I will say this: even good seed is choked by weeds when the sower casts it upon fields that the harrow <note>A farm tool used to break up clods of earth and clear weeds before sowing; here used as a metaphor for preparing the mind for moral growth.</note> has not yet cleared. Man easily allows his heart to make a plaything of him. Self-interest and self-love are our mainsprings <term>Triebfedern: literally "driving springs," referring to the internal mechanics or motivations of human behavior.</term>; they soon degenerate into evil if vigilance over ourselves does not impartially reveal our hearts and make us strict judges of our own actions.

The calming of the passions is the first step toward wisdom. The sun’s image is not reflected in a murky brook; the spring must be pure, and no storm must stir if the sun is to see its reflection within it. Consider these principles, young men! And then, judge yourselves. For the sake of humanity and your own well-being, I will—

—I [will] share my principles with you, dear friends of my fatherland! I gathered them from nature—from experience, from an understanding of human nature <term>Menschenkenntniß: a key Enlightenment concept referring to the practical psychological knowledge of how people think, feel, and behave.</term>. If you find that what I tell you is not in the nature of things, then call me a fool and laugh at me as a simpleton; but if I tell you things that are grounded in the knowledge of human nature, in pure philosophy, and in noble intentions for all of humanity, then I beg you: do not be deaf to my words!

Consider! He who speaks to you has meant well by you; he is himself still a contemporary <note>original: *Mitgespann eurer Jahren*, literally "yoke-mate of your years," meaning a peer or someone of the same generation.</note> of your years; he is no grumbling old man whose gray hairs might make his message suspect to you. Youthful blood still flows in his veins; his heart still beats with the gentle feelings of human joys. He is not ashamed if his cheeks are wetted by a tear while reading a sentimental book, or if a teardrop rises to his eye during a moving tragedy <note>original: *Trauerspiel*, the German term for a tragic play or drama.</note>. He lives among you in society; he is a citizen, a husband, and a father. He knows the feelings of friendship and of love; his self-interest is interwoven with your own; his principles are those he wishes to impart to the most precious gifts of nature—his children. And could you, despite all this, [doubt] his—

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When I hear mention of Enlightenment <term>Enlightenment: original *Aufklärung*, the intellectual movement emphasizing reason and individual rights over tradition and dogma.</term>, it
seems to me that the man who utters this word
intends to speak of the well-being of humanity;

this: we wish to teach men to know their true
worth, their great destiny <term>destiny: original *Bestimmung*, which can also mean "vocation" or "purpose."</term>;
we wish to search for the sources of their
misery, to find their foundations, and we
wish to seal these sources.

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the author's emotional personal appeal to a structured, axiomatic defense of Enlightenment principles. It picks up from the previous page's conclusion where the author urged the reader to listen with an open heart.</meta>

-><image-desc>A typographical vignette or ornament at the top of the page, featuring a central floral or shell-like motif flanked by horizontal decorative lines and scrolls.</image-desc><-

Everything that distances man from the love of humanity <term>love of humanity: original *Menschenliebe*, a central Enlightenment concept similar to modern philanthropy but implying a deeper moral and spiritual kinship with all people.</term>, distances him from his happiness: everything that brings him closer to the love of humanity, brings him closer to his happiness. To spread brotherly love among mortals must always be the motive of the Enlightener <term>Enlightener: original *Aufklärer*, one who actively works to spread the "light" of reason and education.</term>, and brotherly love must be its consequence.

## I therefore form the following propositions:

To enlighten one’s fellow man means to lead him closer to the light; to lead him closer to the light means to let man more thoroughly understand the welfare of humanity, and this insight consists in the knowledge of brotherly love.

## Further propositions.

Self-interest and self-love are the primary drivers <term>drivers: original *Haupttriebfedern*, literally "mainsprings," a common 18th-century mechanical metaphor for human psychology.</term> of men; if they are guided well, they lead to the good; if they are not guided well, they lead to evil. Human society is a union of people for the social welfare. From this union arise laws. Laws are necessary relations arising from the nature of things <note>original: "Geseze sind nothwendige, aus der Natur der Sache entstehende Verhältnisse." This is a direct reference to the famous opening of Montesquieu's *The Spirit of the Laws* (1748).</note>. Laws of society are necessary relations arising from the nature of society. What is in accordance with these relations is social—

<summary>The author presents a series of philosophical axioms defining Enlightenment as the pursuit of "brotherly love" and "the welfare of humanity." He argues that laws are not arbitrary but are necessary relationships derived from the nature of society itself.</summary>

<keywords>Enlightenment, brotherly love, philanthropy, self-interest, social welfare, nature of laws, Montesquieu, social contract</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical deduction regarding the nature of social laws, building directly on the Montesquieu-inspired definition of laws as "necessary relations" from the previous page.</meta>

good; what is not in accordance with these relations,
is socially evil.

Private self-interest <term>self-interest: original *Eigennutz*, often discussed by Enlightenment thinkers like Adam Smith or Helvetius as a primary motivator that must be harnessed for the public good.</term> and self-love separated 
men from one another and were the sources of 
their misfortune.

Communal self-interest and love for the 
whole united them and were the primary foundation 
of the social virtues.

When private self-interest and self-love 
overstep their bounds, then social 
relations suffer; when private self-interest and 
self-love are linked with the interest and the love of 
all humanity, then society gains.

### Conclusions.

Therefore, whatever unites private self-interest and 
self-love with the interest and the love of all 
humanity contributes to the welfare of 
society, and this contribution is an approach 
toward human happiness, and thus an approach toward the light 
and toward Enlightenment.

<vocab>private self-interest, self-love, social, virtues, humanity, human happiness, light, Enlightenment</vocab>

<summary>This page defines social good and evil based on how individual self-interest aligns with the collective welfare of humanity. It concludes that the goal of Enlightenment is to harmonize these private desires with the broader interests of society to achieve universal happiness.</summary>
<keywords>self-interest, self-love, social virtue, human happiness, Enlightenment, social contract, ethics, collective welfare</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical deduction from page 13, moving from the definition of social virtues to a direct correlation between collective well-being and the Enlightenment itself. It maintains the catchword "Was" (What/That which) from the bottom of the previous page.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative typographical divider consisting of a central ornamental flourish flanked by horizontal lines.</image-desc>

---

That which separates private self-interest and the self-love of the individual from the interest and the love of all humanity is a departure from human happiness, and thus an approach toward darkness.

Philanthropy <note>The German *Menschenliebe* (literally "love of humans") was a central concept in 18th-century ethics, often translated as philanthropy or brotherly love, signifying a practical concern for the welfare of all people.</note> unites the private interest and the self-love of the individual with the interest and the love of all humanity.

Philanthropy is therefore an approach toward human happiness and toward Enlightenment.

### Further Conclusions.

Therefore, whatever leads to universal philanthropy leads to Enlightenment; whatever moves away from universal philanthropy moves away from Enlightenment; whatever links the private interest of the individual with the interest of all humanity leads to Enlightenment.

Whatever separates private interest from the interest of all humanity moves away from Enlightenment. Whatever individual self-love produces without love for the whole is darkness <note>In the metaphorical language of the era, "darkness" (*Finsterniß*) represents ignorance, prejudice, and the absence of reason.</note>.

<vocab>private self-interest, self-love, philanthropy, human happiness, Enlightenment, darkness</vocab>

<summary>This page defines Enlightenment as the alignment of individual self-love with the collective good of humanity. It argues that philanthropy serves as the essential bridge that leads society away from "darkness" and toward universal happiness.</summary>
<keywords>philanthropy, self-interest, Enlightenment, human happiness, ethics, collective interest, social harmony</keywords>

The great secret of the Enlightenment of our fellow human beings therefore consists in this maxim: Seek to unite the private interest and self-love <note>In the 18th century, *Selbstliebe* (self-love) was often distinguished from selfishness; it was seen as a natural drive for self-preservation that, when balanced by reason, benefits society.</note> of the individual with the interest and love of all humanity.

->Whatever leads man toward the fulfillment of these maxims<-
->leads to Enlightenment.<-

The actions of men are guided by two fundamental impulses: by final ends and by means.

Final ends are the objects that one desires to attain:

Means are the tools through which one attains them. The final end of Enlightenment is human happiness; the means are Philosophy and Religion.

Philosophy leads the spirit of inquiry <note>The German *Forschgeist* refers to an active, curious, and scientific mind.</note> to knowledge, and guides the understanding. Religion elevates that knowledge, and guides the heart.

<vocab>self-love, Enlightenment, fellow humans, private interest, humanity, fundamental impulses, final ends, means, human happiness, philosophy, religion, spirit of inquiry, knowledge, understanding, cognition, heart</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the "secret" of Enlightenment as the harmonious unification of individual self-interest with the collective welfare of humanity. It identifies human happiness as the ultimate goal of this movement, achieved through the dual tools of philosophy and religion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 15, which concluded that philosophy and religion are the primary means to achieve human happiness. The catchword "Phi-" from the previous page correctly anticipates the first word of this page, "Philosophie."</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal divider ornament featuring a central scroll motif flanked by symmetrical leafy flourishes.</image-desc>

Philosophy and religion must therefore be united in Enlightenment, for philosophy without religion runs the risk of degenerating into free-thinking <term>Freydenkerey: A 18th-century term for a perceived reckless or atheistic rejection of religious and moral foundations.</term>, and religion without philosophy into superstition. True philosophy and true religion are compatible; true religion and false philosophy (or free-thinking) are not compatible; true philosophy and false religion (or superstition) are also not compatible. From all of this follows that true Enlightenment must consist in the union of true religion with true philosophy.

I establish these principles before proceeding further, and I reflect with horror on so many incorrect concepts by which most "Enlighteners" in our century lead the youth astray. Under the pretext of improvement, people attack everything with extreme haste, tear everything down, and put nothing in the place of what has been destroyed. They confuse religion with superstition, and free-thinking with philosophy; they dispute error, yet they persecute the truth. While our understanding is constantly seeking light, our heart goes astray in darkness; we become the plaything of our passions and the victim of our desires. Light

It is not difficult to investigate whether truth is the true object of an Enlightener <term>Aufklärer: A scholar or writer of the Enlightenment era (the Aufklärung) who sought to spread knowledge and reason to the public.</term>, provided that the person reading the Enlightener's writings is not "sick in their soul."

The true Enlightener <term>Aufklärer: A scholar or thinker of the Enlightenment era who sought to spread knowledge and reason.</term> is mostly gentle; he shows consideration toward everyone; he attacks vice, but spares the person. He observes his own heart, is always watchful over his spirit, forces his opinions on no one, has no mania for proselytizing <term>Proselyten-Sucht: An obsessive urge to convert others to one's own viewpoint or beliefs.</term>, but rather leaves the victory to truth itself. He is not proud of his opinions, nor passionate in his criticism. His intention is to lead people to their happiness, and his proof is his own experience: that wisdom consists in the victory over our passions. 

Yes, wisdom—for enlightenment is indeed supposed to lead us to wisdom. Yes—wisdom is only found in the heart that is freed from stormy passions. To approach wisdom means to wrench oneself free from the servitude of the passions; and to "be light" means to scatter the darknesses of our soul that prevent us from finding light where light exists.

This principle is indeed correct. Truth is generally not found where passion is: it is therefore also correct that Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung: The 18th-century intellectual movement emphasizing reason and individualism over tradition.</term> cannot exist where passion is; for Enlightenment is the approach toward human happiness, and this approach occurs through the knowledge of truth.

When I now judge the writings of our "Enlighteners" in our current era, I first look to see whether truth is in the book I am reading; for where truth is not, there is no Enlightenment, and where passion is, truth is seldom found. I do not immediately imagine the author to be a philosopher, but rather I want to see first whether he deserves the title he claims for himself. I place him back among the "natural men" <term>Naturmenschen: Humans in their raw, uncivilized state, driven by basic instincts and emotions rather than refined reason.</term>, return his passions to him, and see how much his self-love, how much his own interest has influenced his scribbling <note>original: "Schreiberey" — a slightly derogatory term for writing or "pen-pushing."</note>: then I observe closely to what extent his upbringing and his temperament reveal themselves in his style of writing, for writing is the physiognomy <term>Physiognomik: The practice of judging character or personality from outward appearance; here, the author suggests that one's writing style reveals the inner "face" of their soul.</term> of the soul, and a sharp eye

There is a language of the intellect <term>Verstand: The faculty of logical, analytical, and rational thought, often contrasted in this period with the emotional "heart."</term>, a language of wit <term>Witz: In the 18th century, "wit" referred to mental sharpness and the ability to make clever, often biting, associations.</term>, and a language of the heart. In the language of the intellect, the heart sometimes has no share; but with the language of the heart, the intellect is mostly united. The language of wit is often the interpreter of an evil heart.

Once I have placed my author in all these situations, I subtract from his work whatever upbringing, temperament, the circumstances in which he wrote, and his situation contributed to it; what remains, I weigh with the lead-weight of truth <note>original: "Bleywaage der Wahrheit" — a metaphor for a heavy, uncompromising, and exact standard of measurement, like a plumb line used in construction.</note>: whatever withstands this test is good; whatever does not withstand it is evil. *

---

* If the writer does not benefit humanity, if greed or misanthropic pride guides his pen, and insults fill his pages; then the person of feeling sadly sets the book aside, and a tear of compassion wets his eye; his heart is constricted, and with a gaze turned toward heaven, he sighs over the writer’s foul mood.

<summary>The author distinguishes between the languages of the intellect, wit, and the heart, arguing that true writing must be stripped of personal bias to be measured against the "lead-weight of truth." <meta>This page follows the author's critique of the traveler Friedrich Nicolai. -><image-desc>A decorative horizontal divider consisting of a central flourish resembling a stylized fleur-de-lis flanked by two horizontal lines on each side.</image-desc><-

It is easy to guess in our century why a multitude of the best young men so eagerly devour all those dangerous books and pamphlets that poison their souls to the detriment of the state and humanity. One need only return to the human heart. Young men are without experience, without close observation of their own hearts, without knowledge of the world or of people: their self-love easily misleads them into pride; pride leads to contempt for all things that are common <note>In this context, "common" refers to the shared beliefs and traditions of the general public, which the elite "freethinkers" looked down upon.</note>. The love of the singular is the child of pride. One believes oneself to be smarter, one believes oneself to be wiser, if one does not think as people generally think; if one does not believe as people generally believe; and this is the first foundation for freethinking. Conviction is not there; for conviction only exists alongside truth, and truth is not where pride is, and pride is not where reason is.

Proud youth! You who have read the immortal works of the ancients; you who know what <term>Helvétius: Claude Adrien Helvétius (1715–1771), a French philosopher whose materialistic and atheistic views were often condemned as dangerous to public morality.</term> thought, what <term>Mirabeau: Likely referring to the Marquis de Mirabeau, a French physiocrat, or the radical ideas associated with the Mirabeau name prior to the French Revolution.</term> dreamed; you who possess strength of spirit, agility in thinking, and courage in execution! You, an angel in intellect, a creator in thoughts!

**Vocabulary**
*   **Youths**: Young men (*Jünglinge*)
*   **Pamphlets**: Small, often controversial booklets (*Brochüren*)
*   **Freethinking**: The rejection of traditional religious dogma in favor of reason (*Freydenkerey*)
*   **Pride**: Hubris or vanity (*Stolz*)
*   **Reason**: The capacity for rational thought (*Vernunft*)
*   **Helvétius**: A controversial French Enlightenment philosopher
*   **Mirabeau**: A prominent French noble and political thinker

<summary>The author analyzes the psychological roots of "freethinking" among the youth, attributing their attraction to radical Enlightenment literature to a mix of inexperience and intellectual pride. He argues that the desire to feel superior to the "common" people leads young men to reject traditional truths in favor of dangerous new ideas.</summary>

now it is so easily said: in just a few words, the youth finds the entirety of philosophy, which the wise man cannot find even through mature reflection during many sleepless nights. We have demonstrated thus far that the great secret of the Enlightenment consists in uniting the private interest and self-love of the individual with the interest and love of all humanity.

To enlighten means to unite pure philosophy with true religion.

An Enlightener is one who unites pure philosophy with true religion, and a people is enlightened where pure philosophy is united with true religion.

### Inferences.

Whatever prevents pure philosophy from uniting with religion hinders Enlightenment; whatever prevents true religion from uniting with pure philosophy hinders Enlightenment. The philosopher who hates religion is an enemy of Enlightenment. The religious person who hates philosophy is an enemy of Enlightenment.

<meta>This page continues the classification of "goods" (Güter) introduced on page 34, specifically detailing the hierarchy from sensory pleasures to intellectual and moral achievements.</meta>

The first are merely pleasant sensations of the senses; the second are goods of the intellect <note>original: "Güter des Verstandes."</note>, such as: taste, knowledge, memory, and the power of judgment.

The latter consist in the governance of the dominant passions through reason and experience, and in their wise application for the welfare of humanity.

These gradual experiences, which man acquired upon entering into social life, soon made him more attentive, and the natural impulse of his self-love <note>original: "Selbstliebe." In 18th-century philosophy, this refers to a natural instinct for self-preservation and improvement, rather than vanity.</note> soon led him to the discovery of further goods.

In the situation in which he found himself, he first learned to recognize physical goods through comparison.

Sickness taught him the value of health; weakness the value of strength; clumsiness <note>original: "Plumpheit."</note> the value of agility <note>original: "Hurtigkeit."</note>; and uncleanness the value of cleanliness.

Correct application will provide the happiness that you seek; incorrect application of your powers will distance you from it. You surely see, then, that there must be even higher secrets, and that you must have a guide on the path I have laid out for your happiness.

I want to teach you to know this guide: it is your heart.

You will not yet understand me, but my obscure language will become clearer to you when I show you where they will lead you.

The happiness of humanity is the great destiny; the happiness of the individual consists in the happiness of the whole <note>This reflects the Enlightenment ideal of cosmopolitanism and utilitarianism—the idea that one's personal well-being is inextricably linked to the well-being of the community.</note>.

Step, therefore, into the Sanctuary of Nature, and read the inscription: Man! act toward your fellow man as you wish that your fellow man should act toward you.

In society you are master or servant; ruler or subject, father or son, brother or sister, husband or wife; your heart will tell you what your duties are. For what your self-love <note>original: "Selbstliebe." Thus the Divinity once spoke in the Temple of Nature, and Reason proclaimed its voice to mankind.

He to whom it was audible inscribed its words deep into his heart; and he who remained faithful to this voice was the priest of nature, the first philosopher.

However, the ordinary course of the human heart made the multitude deaf to the call of the Divinity.

Self-love pitted people against one another. Self-interest <note>original: "Eigennuz." This refers to seeking personal gain at the expense of the collective good.</note> separated private advantages from the advantages of humanity.

Passion triumphed over Reason and drowned out the pure voice of philosophy.

<meta>This page continues the argument that true philosophy and true religion are naturally aligned. While the previous page blamed superstition for the historical persecution of philosophy, this page shifts the focus to "free-thinking" as the contemporary threat that wrongly positions itself as the enemy of faith. It sets up a structured investigation into the nature of both concepts, moving from general definitions to the specifics of divine revelation. The catchword "blinde" (blind) at the bottom of the page links to the discussion of "blind fate" that concludes the text.</meta>

Thus true philosophy never attacks religion; for pure philosophy is compatible with true religion: only free-thinking <term>Freygeisterey: A term used in the 18th century to describe radical rationalism or deism, often carrying a negative connotation of intellectual arrogance or moral laxity.</term> is incompatible, and free-thinking is not philosophy.

It will be necessary here for us to investigate what free-thinking and what religion actually are. First, we wish to speak in general terms; then, once the dignity of religion and its beneficial influence on the well-being of humanity have been proven, we shall take Revelation <term>Offenbarung: The belief in truths revealed directly by God to humanity, such as through scripture, as opposed to truths found through human reason alone.</term> alone as our focus and judge its essential goodness impartially; reason shall convince, while the heart and nature shall provide the proof.

Generally, those philosophers are called free-thinkers—who are so common among us today—who deny the existence of a Supreme Being or Providence <note>original: "Vorsicht," an archaic term for "Vorsehung" or Divine Providence—the belief that God actively guides the universe.</note>, and believe that everything is matter. They say that a certain quality of this matter, which is the human soul, is also the soul of the world and of all things. Through it, the immense weights of the celestial bodies <note>original: "Weltkörper," literally "world-bodies."</note> roll above us, and the plants bloom through it beneath our feet. As a consequence of this teaching, they assert that a blind fate rules everything; this

<summary>The author argues that true philosophy and religion are compatible, distinguishing them from "free-thinking," which is defined here as a materialistic denial of God and Providence. The page outlines a plan to prove the value of religion and revelation using both reason and emotion.</summary>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of various schools of "free-thinking" philosophy that were viewed as threats to traditional religious belief in the 18th century. It picks up from the previous page's discussion of materialism, moving into a critique of fatalism (the denial of free will) and a form of deism that views God as indifferent to human affairs.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal divider consisting of a central stylized floral or scroll ornament flanked by thin double lines.</image-desc>

blind fate, they claim, has bound events with a chain that nothing can break. Man, who has no freedom, is nothing but a passive <note>original: "leidendes," which in this philosophical context means "passive" or "acted upon," the opposite of "active."</note> tool; he becomes that which destiny wills him to be; he is set in motion by the objects that make an impression on him, just as the clouds are moved by the winds. He decides nothing at all; he only believes that he does so. He determines nothing for himself; he is determined, and therefore, in regards to him, there is neither moral good nor moral evil, neither right nor wrong; in a word, everything is the same to him, except for pain and pleasure, which either torment or tickle his senses. Another kind of these sects <term>Secten: Here referring to schools of philosophical thought rather than modern religious cults.</term> does indeed revere the Supreme Being, yet they do not wish for it to stoop so low as to cast its gaze down to the earth. Though we are a work of the Deity, they say we do not deserve that it should occupy itself with us. It is up to us, they say, to arrange our lives as best we can and to diminish our evils. We have nothing to expect from God; our soul is condemned to death, just like the body, as soon as the organs that set it in motion are worn out by time or destroyed by disease.

**Vocabulary:**
Fate, Freedom, Destiny, Moral Good, Sects, Deity, Soul

<summary>This page outlines two radical philosophical positions: fatalism, which denies human free will and morality, and a form of deism that claims God is indifferent to humanity and that the soul is mortal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Enlightenment-era skepticism and materialism. While the previous page discussed the philosophical errors of fatalism—the belief that humans are passive tools of destiny—this page transitions into the moral consequences of these beliefs, specifically identifying them as the "principles of free-thinking."</meta>

43

<image-desc>A decorative printers ornament (vignette) is centered at the top of the page, featuring symmetrical scrolled and floral motifs.</image-desc>

These are the principles of **free-thinking** <term>Freygeisterey: In the 18th century, this term was often used pejoratively by religious writers to describe skeptics, deists, or atheists who rejected church authority and traditional morality.</term>. The system behind it is not difficult to outline. The lowest sort of person, who gives himself over to his passions undisturbed, preaches it through his actions, and his actions can easily be distilled into principles. They are approximately these:

Seek your **pleasure**; whatever promotes this is permitted and wise; whatever keeps you from it is folly, timidity, and superstition.

**Self-love** <note>original: "Selbstliebe," here referring to self-interest or egoism.</note> is your law; follow it as long as no overt force stops you, and fear nothing but the arm of power <note>Likely referring to the power of the state or the law.</note>.

Nothing is good in itself, and nothing is evil.

The **Deity** does not heed the lowly actions of men, and man’s nature commands him to act according to his implanted instinct.

He is **free** who is permitted to do what he desires, and only that which he desires constitutes his happiness.

The pleasures of the **senses** and of the **imagination**, the joys of lust, of honor, and of wealth.

This system, says **Gellert** <note>Christian Fürchtegott Gellert (1715–1769) was one of the most widely read German authors of the Enlightenment, known for his moral fables and his efforts to reconcile reason with religious sentiment.</note>, deserves no refutation; it excites disgust as soon as one considers its consequences, and the heart that is not entirely corrupted revolts with its natural goodness against the insolence of **unbelief**.

How wretched the **freethinker** would be, he continues, if he could transform a **republic** of men into such philosophers as he himself is, or wishes to be. How would it stand with his idolized pleasure, with the possession of the goods and persons that he requires for his wishes, with his security and his life? I and all others would then be minded like him. We too would know no difference between good and evil. Our god is **self-interest** <note>original: "Eigennuz."</note>, **self-love** <note>original: "Selbstliebe." In 18th-century moral philosophy, this often referred to narrow egoism rather than healthy self-esteem.</note>, and the pleasure of the senses.

Will we not snatch his joys from him with cunning or force, as soon as our pleasure commands it? What does his peace matter to me, if I can promote my own through the destruction of his? I rob him of it. But he will resist? So I resist as well. After it has been well-impressed upon them that there is neither right nor wrong, neither virtue nor vice, and they have reached the point where they can read the proof of these truths even in some of their own admirable works, then the educators will be directed by law to neglect nothing in order to protect them against the prejudices of ignorance and superstition, and to accustom them to a strong and vigorous logic which is in no way intimidated by some of its occasionally shocking consequences. <note>The "shocking consequences" likely refer to the abandonment of traditional social taboos or the justification of selfish behavior in the name of reason.</note>

—set forth by **Bishop Pontoppidan** <term>Erik Pontoppidan (1698–1764)|A Danish bishop and theologian whose work "The Power of Faith to Overcome Unbelief" was a key text in the defense of Christianity against Enlightenment skepticism.</term> in his work: *The Power to Overcome Unbelief*.

The pure intention with which religion sanctifies **virtue** <term>Tugend|The author views virtue not just as good behavior, but as a holy state achieved through divine grace.</term> is already the hallmark of its dignity. The shimmer of light in philosophy, says **Gellert**, is a bright midday in religion. Everything flows together into the center, everything in God. The divine attributes themselves are, in religion, motives for virtue and deterrents from vice; **natural religion** <term>Natürliche Religion|Philosophy based on reason and nature alone, without the aid of holy scripture or divine "revelation."</term> and philosophy are not capable, without **revelation**, of recognizing the characteristics of virtue in a clear light.

The love for God, he continues, which is generated by the faith that we—notwithstanding all our guilt—have been made infinitely happy through the merit of a divine **Savior**, animates the heart with a divine power to overcome evil inclinations; it spreads benevolence and love toward all people.

The great examples of virtue that religion presents to us in the image of the Savior; its power, through which it leads our heart and our understanding to virtue; all its effects—

—even upon the crudest hearts, there are proofs of its excellence, and proofs that **human happiness** <term>Menschenglück|A central 18th-century concept referring to the flourishing and well-being of the human race.</term> is bound up with its **principles** <term>Grundsätze|The fundamental moral and spiritual laws upon which a system is built.</term>.

It is certain that that **freedom** <term>Freyheit|In this context, the author uses the term critically to mean the intellectual license or "shamelessness" used to critique faith.</term> with which one attacks **religion** so shamelessly today—a religion which one does not even know—is one of the greatest misfortunes of **Europe**. I am by no means a **theologian**; but I say only this: Even if religion were not as holy as we have proven it to be, if it did not produce all those effects for human happiness that we have mentioned;

—Even assuming our religion were a mere invention, like the religion of the ancients, everyone must still confess that in the current state of affairs it is the only **moral compass** <term>Richtschnur der Moral|Literally a "measuring line" or "guiding rule" for ethical behavior.</term> that the majority of people possess—people who, without it, would know no other bridle for their passions. What are they more than works of stupidity, and testimonies to the **depravity of the heart**?

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of contemporary "freethinkers." After arguing in the previous page that religion serves as a necessary moral compass for society, the author now contrasts the aggressive tactics of modern skeptics with the respectful approach of the philosopher Socrates.</meta>

->❦<-
<note>The original manuscript features a decorative floral ornament here.</note>

Since in our day there is hardly a **freethinker** <term>Freydenker|A term used in the 18th century to describe intellectuals who rejected traditional religious dogma in favor of reason, often used pejoratively by critics of the Enlightenment.</term> who does not, at least in his own imagination, compare himself to **Socrates**, I could only wish that these gentlemen might also truly resemble him.

History tells us of this sage that he spoke of the **Highest Being** <term>höchstes Wesen|A characteristic Enlightenment term for God, emphasizing a rational, supreme creator over specific sectarian definitions.</term> with all the dignity and greatness that can be expected from the human spirit, even though he lived in the midst of **superstition**. Do we ever see him mocking the public religion? Did he incite the **Athenians** to lock up their temples and tear down their altars? Was it perhaps by his counsel that **Alcibiades** <note>A brilliant but controversial Athenian politician and pupil of Socrates; he was accused of profaning the Mysteries and mutilating the statues of Hermes (Mercury) in 415 BCE.</note> mutilated the statues of **Mercury**? Did the foolish notion ever occur to him to declare himself an enemy of **Jupiter** or **Minerva**?

He never declaimed against the gods of the Athenians; he was content to reveal the truth by speaking of the wisdom and all the other attributes of the Highest Being. He desired that the Greeks should first come to know and revere that God whom the whole world must worship, before they came to loathe the service of a **Saturn** or a **Jupiter**, and renounced the fables which the creative **imagination** <term>Einbildungskraft|The faculty of the mind that produces images and myths; here the author suggests that ancient polytheism was a product of poetic fancy rather than divine truth.</term>

<summary>The author contrasts modern anti-religious "freethinkers" with Socrates, arguing that the ancient philosopher respected public religious institutions while gently teaching a higher rational truth. This serves as a critique of Enlightenment radicals who sought to dismantle religious structures entirely.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Freethinker, Highest Being, Athens, Alcibiades, Polytheism, Enlightenment Philosophy, Religious Tolerance</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the author's comparison between the historical Socrates and modern Enlightenment skeptics. It follows a passage arguing that Socrates did not seek to destroy the religious foundations of Athens, but rather to refine them gently.</meta>

...that the poets created. To put it in a single word: he loved and nurtured in his fellow citizens that sense of **piety** <term>Gottesfurcht|Literally "fear of God"; in this context, it refers to a deep-seated religious reverence or "fear of the Lord" that serves as a social glue.</term> which bound them firmly to their superstitious customs. He hoped to make use of this foundation to lead them toward a more rational religion without causing offense, unrest, or endangering public morals.

Since those who today pass themselves off as students of **Socrates** neither know his principles nor follow his actions, it is necessary to regard them as dreamers whose teachings are beneficial to no human society.

"I leave the task of curing such men," says **Mably** <note>Gabriel Bonnot de Mably (1709–1785), a French philosopher and social critic. Though often radical, he believed religion was an essential instrument for maintaining public morality.</note>, "to the law; but the legislators should consider," he adds, "should consider that it is better to prescribe **hellebore** <term>Nießwurz|A plant used in ancient medicine as a treatment for madness or melancholy.</term> to such men than to have them take **hemlock** <note>The poison used to execute Socrates. The author is suggesting that modern "freethinkers" are not martyrs like Socrates, but merely insane individuals who need medicine rather than execution.</note>."

It is indeed worthy of the **madhouse** to want to erase the doctrine of religion from humanity—the very thing that leads man to happiness—only to put in its place a system of teaching that would fill the world with villains.

<meta>This page transitions from the author's previous historical comparison between Socrates and modern skeptics to a new section using "natural reason" to justify religious and social structures. However, it shall not yet be enough that we have proven the harmfulness of **freethinking** <term>Freydenkerey|An 18th-century term for the movement that rejected religious dogma in favor of independent reason, often viewed by traditionalists as a threat to social order.</term> through its exposure, through its consequences, and through the portraits that the most excellent men have drawn of it; it shall also not be enough that we have explained the dignity of **revealed religion** through its teachings, through examples, and through authority; we wish to explain it even more thoroughly through **natural reason** <term>natürliche Vernunft|The human capacity for logic and moral judgment independent of divine revelation, a key concept in Enlightenment philosophy.</term>.

## P r o p o s i t i o n s.

Self-interest and self-love are the fundamental drives of human beings in their actions. From their powerful instinct, the passions arise. Man pants for their satisfaction and seeks his happiness in this satisfaction.

Man without education <note>The original German "Erziehung" in this era referred not just to schooling, but to the entire moral and social upbringing required to civilize a person.</note> is like the savages; he possesses only sensory feelings and measures his bliss according to the greater or lesser degree of enjoyment.

Only when man entered into society did he become acquainted with higher goods, and felt the necessity of subordinating the goods of sensory feelings to the goods of the intellect.

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Propositions" section from page 71. The previous page ended with a discussion on how humans in society must subordinate sensory feelings to the intellect. Although the previous catchword was "Er=", this page begins with "Erfahrung" (Experience), continuing the investigation into how human understanding and motives develop.</meta>

Experience taught him the knowledge of the goods of the body; he felt their weakness through the consequences of immoderate enjoyment, and this insight made him more attentive, so that he began to distinguish real goods from **apparent goods** <term>Scheingüter|A philosophical concept referring to things that seem beneficial or pleasurable at first glance but are ultimately deceptive or harmful to one's moral well-being.</term>.

Man acts according to the nature of his knowledge; this knowledge is guided by his **concepts** <term>Begriffe|In this context, the mental frameworks or categories through which an individual perceives and judges the world.</term>, and therefore becomes erroneous or true, just as the concepts themselves are.

Man never acts without a **motive** <term>Beweggrund|Literally a "ground for movement"; the internal drive or reason that prompts a person to act.</term>; this motive is for the most part self-interest and self-love. And because self-interest and self-love always prioritize our own worthy person over our fellow man, this is the cause of all hostile inclinations and the conflict of interests from which all social vices spring.

Man never chooses evil because it is evil, but always because he perceives it as good; and the deception of the senses and of passion is the cause of his frequent failure to distinguish the **apparent good** from the **true good**.

<meta>This page continues the transition from individual motives (discussed on page 72) to the formation of social structures. While the previous page's catchword was "Der," this page begins with "Die Bedürfnisse" (The needs), describing how necessity drives the formation of society and the definition of natural laws.</meta>

The needs that made **social union** <term>gesellschaftliche Vereinigung|The act of humans coming together to form a community or state, often discussed in Enlightenment philosophy as the "social contract."</term> a necessity for man soon led him to realize that self-interest and private interest must harmonize with the interest and love of the whole.

This harmony produced social virtues; disharmony produced social vices. The former led man toward happiness; the latter distanced him from it.

Those who observed human nature <note>original: "Die Beobachter seiner selbst," literally 'the observers of oneself' or 'the observers of his own kind.'</note> collected the results from experience; and from this collection, people learned to recognize the **necessary relationships** of society—which necessary relationships people called **laws** <term>Geseze|In this context, the author refers to the fundamental moral and social rules that govern human interaction, rather than just legislative statutes.</term>.

What is consistent with these relationships, as we have already said, is socially good; what is not consistent with these relationships is socially bad.

Since this good and this evil lie in the constitution, or the **nature of the thing** <term>Natur der Sache|A philosophical principle suggesting that certain truths or moral laws are inherent in the way the world and human society are naturally structured, rather than being arbitrary human inventions.</term>, we are convinced of what is naturally good and what is naturally evil.

Man possesses natural powers of his being; the manifestation of internal powers, without regard for a **fellow creature** <term>Mitgeschöpf|A term emphasizing that all beings are created together; in this context, it refers to the social existence of humans alongside others.</term>, is called vegetating—is called living <note>The author distinguishes between "living" as a basic biological function and "behaving" as a social action.</note>.

It is good when it accords with natural circumstances; evil when it does not accord;

Indifferent when, although it does not accord with natural circumstances, it also does not contradict them.

From this, we can conclude what is naturally good and what is naturally evil.

The philosopher, the naturalist <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, a "naturalist" referred to a thinker who believed that religious truth could be found through reason and the study of nature alone, without the need for supernatural revelation.</note>, has no other grounds than ever-doubting reason;

**Faith** is a person’s holding-to-be-true <note>German: *Dafürhalten*. This is a specific philosophical term for an internal acceptance of a truth that one cannot personally prove but accepts as valid.</note> of the truth of a matter of which they have not convinced themselves, but which is stated as true by others.

One **knows** when one has clear concepts and knowledge of a matter;

One **believes** when one does not have clear concepts and knowledge of a matter oneself, but rather attributes clear knowledge and concepts to other worthy individuals <note>The author distinguishes between "knowing" (personal intellectual grasp) and "believing" (relying on the testimony and insight of others, such as divinely inspired figures).</note>.

<summary>This page defines the fundamental difference between philosophy and religion, characterizing philosophy as a product of "doubting reason" and religion as a system of "faith" anchored in divine confirmation. It offers specific definitions for knowing versus believing, framing faith as trust in the knowledge of others.</summary>

<image-desc>Centered at the top of the page, beneath the page number, is a decorative horizontal vignette or printer's ornament featuring symmetrical scrolled foliage and a central floral motif.</image-desc>

That revelation contributes to the well-being of humanity is not something one merely believes, for this is something one knows, and of which one can be convinced;

The mysteries <note>German: *Geheimnisse*. In an 18th-century theological context, a "mystery" refers to a divine truth that is above reason and cannot be discovered by the human mind alone, but must be revealed by God.</note> of religion, however, cannot be known; rather, they are believed, because mysteries are hidden from our power of cognition <note>German: *Erkenntnißkraft*. This refers to the human faculty or capacity for acquiring and processing knowledge.</note>.

In order to doubt the mystery of a matter with reason, one must either have proof of the matter’s impossibility, or proof of suspicion against the one who presents the mystery.

We can have neither of these in revealed religion.

We cannot determine the impossibility of a mystery, because determining the impossibility of a thing presupposes the clearest knowledge of all possible things—and how can a human presume such audacity?

We also cannot have proof of suspicion against those who revealed the mysteries, for Christ and the Apostles were entirely human- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "schen=" suggests the completed word is *menschenfreundlich*, meaning "philanthropic" or "loving of humanity."</note>

<vocab>Revelation, Humanity, Mysteries, Religion, Power of Cognition, Reason, Impossibility, Christ, Apostles</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that while the benefits of revelation are observable facts, its mysteries must be accepted through faith. It posits that human reason is insufficient to prove a divine mystery "impossible" because humans do not possess total knowledge of all possibilities.</summary>
<keywords>revelation, divine mysteries, epistemology, faith vs reason, impossibility, Christ, Apostles, cognitive power</keywords>

There is a twofold reason for the incomprehensibility of certain truths, both of natural and of revealed religion. One lies in **God** Himself; the other in the nature of the human understanding and its way of thinking about God.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 84 regarding the reasons for the incomprehensibility of religious truths. The catchword "weil-" (because) from the previous page's final paragraph leads into the explanation of God’s infinite nature.</meta>

85

-><image-desc>A decorative horizontal bar with a central fleur-de-lis ornament.</image-desc><-

God must necessarily be a mysterious being, because we worship an infinite being in Him. He is the highest in all His attributes and actions. But no human being can imagine the highest degree of a thing or a quality with perfect clarity.

Could he do this, he would cease to be limited; he would himself become infinite. Therefore, God must be able to reveal <note>German: *entdecken*. In this context, it refers to the act of "disclosing" or "uncovering" truths that were previously hidden from human reason.</note> much to His creatures that is not entirely comprehensible to them, and perhaps even never will be entirely comprehensible. It is for this very reason probable that, if it pleases Him to give mankind a special **revelation**, there will be **mysteries** within it that exceed our imagination. It is not strictly necessary <note>The author suggests that while a revelation *could* just consist of things we might eventually figure out on our own, it is more likely to contain things beyond our reach.</note>, because a revelation of such truths that we might indeed discover through **reflection** <note>German: *Nachdenken*. This refers to the use of human reason and logic to understand the world and the divine.</note> can still be very useful, because the path of reflection is difficult and is not trodden by all people. Nevertheless, it remains probable, and it follows that a revelation that makes useful mysteries known to us is worthy of God. It accords with the **majesty** and greatness of His nature, and for this very reason we owe it more reverence and even more gratitude.

**Vocabulary**
*   **God** <gloss>Gott</gloss>
*   **Revelation** <gloss>Offenbarung</gloss>
*   **Mystery** <gloss>Geheimniß</gloss>
*   **Infinity** <gloss>Unendlichkeit</gloss>
*   **Reflection** <gloss>Nachdenken</gloss>
*   **Majesty** <gloss>Hoheit</gloss>

<summary>This page argues that because God is infinite and humans are finite, it is logical and fitting that a divine revelation would contain mysteries beyond human comprehension. Such mysteries do not make the revelation less valid; rather, they reflect the true majesty of God's nature.</summary>
<keywords>God, infinity, revelation, human understanding, mysteries, theology, reason vs. faith</keywords>

Another reason for their incomprehensibility lies in the nature of man and his conception of God, of His attributes and actions. All the conceptions we have of Him are of such a nature that they do not immediately grasp that which is in God as it truly is. <meta>This page continues the argument from page 90, completing the final sentence of the previous section regarding the difficult but certain path of morality. The author transitions from positive arguments for religion to a systematic rebuttal of Enlightenment-era skepticism.</meta>

91

alone secure and certain path of **godliness** <gloss>Gottseligkeit</gloss> and **virtue** <gloss>Tugend</gloss>.

Having now proven that **religion** leads to social happiness, that **revelation** <gloss>Offenbarung</gloss> is necessarily grounded in the nature of things, that the **mysteries of faith** <gloss>Glaubengeheimnisse</gloss> possess moral goodness, and that the philosopher can find no rational cause to doubt the truth and holiness of religion, nothing remains but to refute some of the sham arguments <note>original: "Scheingründe," meaning specious or fallacious reasons that appear valid at first glance.</note> of **unbelief** <gloss>Unglaube</gloss>, with which the **free-thinker** <gloss>Freydenker</gloss> for the most part presumes to dispute our holy religion.

I find that among the **free spirits** <gloss>Freygeister</gloss> <note>At the time of writing, "free spirits" and "free-thinkers" were often derogatory terms used by the religious establishment to describe Enlightenment philosophers who questioned Church dogma.</note> there are still very few of that most wicked kind of men who, against the universal voice of nature and **reason** <gloss>Vernunft</gloss>, completely deny the value of virtue.

The **mockeries** <gloss>Spöttereyen</gloss> of most concern revelation, the mysteries of faith, or certain abuses that do not belong to the essence of religion.

<summary>The author concludes his primary defense of religion’s utility and rationality, moving to address and refute the specific criticisms and "mockeries" leveled by Enlightenment free-thinkers. He notes that while these skeptics attack religious mysteries and institutional abuses, few are so radical as to deny the value of virtue itself.</summary>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of religion against Enlightenment "free-thinkers." While page 91 ended by acknowledging that most skeptics do not deny the value of virtue entirely, this page focuses on defending religious mysteries and separating the core "truth" of faith from historical abuses like fanaticism.</meta>

92

<meta>catchword: Should</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal line with a central scrollwork ornament.</image-desc>

As far as the **mysteries of faith** <gloss>Glaubensgeheimnisse</gloss> are concerned, enough has already been said, since we have proven their moral goodness and their credibility. The **Deity** <gloss>Gottheit</gloss> is goodness, and all His laws are love.

The happiness of the beloved lies in the mysteries of nature; why should the happiness of mortals not lie in the mysteries of faith? <note>The author uses a romantic analogy here, suggesting that just as love in nature has its own beautiful secrets, religion should be allowed its own "mysteries" without being dismissed as irrational.</note>

As for the **abuses of religion** <gloss>Mißbräuche der Religion</gloss>, against which the **mockers** <gloss>Spötter</gloss> <note>This likely refers to Enlightenment philosophers like Voltaire, who famously critiqued the institutional Church's corruption.</note> so boldly rail, these abuses are certainly not the works of religion; they are the works of man, who has distanced himself from the light of true religion; they are the children of **fanaticism** <gloss>Fanatismus</gloss>; the sons of **superstition** <gloss>Aberglauben</gloss>.

Is religion any less holy because, in ancient times, fanaticism and superstition obscured it? Is the sun any less the sun when a cloud covers its face and withdraws its beneficial ray of light from mortals? **Truth** <gloss>Wahrheit</gloss> is always truth, and **error** <gloss>Irrthum</gloss> remains always error.

<note>The author employs a classic Enlightenment-era metaphor—the sun—to argue for religion. Usually, the sun represented "Reason," but here he repurposes it to represent "True Religion," arguing that historical violence or corruption are merely passing "clouds."</note>

<summary>The author argues that the "mysteries of faith" are as natural as the mysteries of love and nature, and asserts that historical religious abuses are the fault of human superstition rather than the core truth of religion itself.</summary>

O mankind! Why then do you mix truth with lies, and light with darkness. Where there is light, there cannot be darkness, and where there is darkness, there is no light.

Nature does not allow these opposite things to mix; where there is light, there is an absence of darkness, and where there is darkness, an absence of light.

Therefore, weak mortal, do not present the qualities of darkness as defects of the light. Light remains light; even in the twilight, it only shines closer to or further from humanity.

*) Nowadays, nothing is as strongly contested as **auricular confession** <term>Ohrenbeicht: From the German "ear-confession," referring to the practice of confessing sins privately to a priest.</term>, and yet it is strange that one could fail to recognize its beneficial influence on **morality**, even if one considers the matter only from a philosophical perspective.

Now, how is it possible for the common man to approach this perfection if he is without a guide? The **confessor** should be the guide of the penitent; he should search his heart and provide him with the means to overcome his dominant passion, which he can easily identify through the confession. What strength the penitent would gain through this, and how securely he would be guided on the path of virtue!

The objection that might be raised—that this happens very rarely—does not weaken my arguments; the fault in this lies not with auricular confession itself, but with the **confessors**. —

To be sure, it would be desirable for the teachers of the people to be held in higher esteem than they have been until now, and that one should not [allow] everyone who leaves their studies, speaks a little **Latin** <note>original: "Latein"</note>, and [memorizes/resolves] a few **cases** <note>original: "Casus" — This likely refers to "cases of conscience" (Casus conscientiae), a branch of theology dealing with specific moral dilemmas, used to train priests for the confessional.</note>...

establishes, and without which that which reason and nature say would long remain insufficient for our happiness. Scripture, the Holy Fathers, <note>original: "h. Vätter" (heilige Väter) — the early influential Christian teachers and bishops known as the Church Fathers.</note> and all those who wrote about the way and means to attain bliss <note>original: "Glückseligkeit" — a state of profound well-being and moral fulfillment, a key concept in the Enlightenment.</note> and virtue have said all of this far more gloriously and more excellently than all the philosophers.

What, then, in the face of all these convincing truths, can still prevent a person from recognizing the holiness of religion and its effect on human happiness?

I conclude, and leave my propositions to everyone to consider; I am no theologian, no scriptural scholar, and even if I were, I would deliberately choose not to provide proof through Scripture and theology. I prove through reason and through experience. If people are bold enough to reject the pronouncements of Scripture and the pronouncements of rational theologians, they will nonetheless be unable to contradict reason and experience. Truth always asserts its rights, even if falsehood had heaped mountains of sand upon it and rolled great boulders over it.

My beloved of my fatherland! <note>original: "Vaterland" — while often translated as "fatherland," in this 18th-century context, it refers to one’s local state or the broader German cultural identity before the existence of a unified Germany.</note> Do not forget your dignity, and you shall not forget it if you hold fast to what I have told you.

## On
# the Literary Intolerance
## of Our Century.

An academic speech delivered in a public assembly by Karl von Eckartshausen, in Munich on April 5, 1785. <note>Karl von Eckartshausen (1752–1803) was a prominent Bavarian philosopher and theosophist. This speech was given during a period of intense intellectual conflict in Bavaria between Enlightenment reformers and traditionalists.</note>

---

> Ignorance has never done harm, error alone is fatal; and one does not go astray because one does not know, but because one believes one knows. <note>original French: "L'ignorance n'a jamais fait de mal, l'erreur seule est funeste; et on ne s'egare point parce qu'on ne fait pas; mais parce qu'on croit savoir."</note>
>
> ->J. J. Rousseau.<-

Here in this magnificent lecture hall, <note>original: "Hörsaale" — a formal room for teaching or public oration.</note> which the goodness of our Prince <note>Referring to Karl Theodor, the Elector of Bavaria.</note> has built for the sciences <note>In the 18th century, "sciences" (Wissenschaften) referred broadly to all branches of systematic knowledge, including the humanities and philosophy.</note>—here, amidst the admirable proportions of the works of the human spirit; in the presence of the scholars of our fatherland, and in your presence, you departed scholars, whose immortal writings stand all around for eternity in this library, <note>original: "Büchersaale" — literally "book hall."</note> in your presence, I say, you! who, freed from the shell of the body, contemplate the works of the Deity in a purer light,

<summary>This page serves as the title and opening invocation of an academic speech by Karl von Eckartshausen attacking the intellectual intolerance of his era. He begins by addressing a distinguished audience of living scholars and the "spirits" of the great authors housed in the Munich library.</summary>
