--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening address of Karl von Eckartshausen's 1785 speech. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing the presence of departed scholars in the library of the Academy.</meta>

...who strived, and whose transfigured spirits hover invisibly over us to attend this solemn ceremony of the foundation anniversary <term>Stiftungsfeyer: A formal celebration marking the establishment of an institution; here, the Bavarian Academy of Sciences.</term> of our Academy—in the presence of you all, I will dare to speak about the literary intolerance of our century.

I know well the criticism to which I expose myself, and the irreconcilable hatred of many journalists and critics I face—men who have hitherto insulted literature, dishonored the sciences <note>In the 18th century, "sciences" (Wissenschaften) referred to all branches of systematic knowledge, including philosophy and the humanities.</note>, and desecrated reason. But what does it matter to me? The upright man seeks the approval for his undertaking in the hearts of the few well-meaning people <note>original: "Gutdenkenden" — a term often used in the Enlightenment to describe those with noble intentions and sound judgment.</note>, and the reward for his actions in the contentment of his own soul.

They may brand me with insults in their journals and, like executioners' assistants <note>original: "Henkersknechte" — literally the henchmen who assisted an executioner, emphasizing the brutality Eckartshausen expects from his critics.</note>, lash me with the scourge of their criticism in their scholarly newspapers; I will not grieve over this, for the truth of my propositions will only be further strengthened, and their offended pride will prove their intolerance all the more strikingly.

<vocab>litterarische Intoleranz: literary intolerance, Stiftungsfeyer: foundation anniversary, Akademie: academy, Journalschreiber: journal-writers/journalists, Kritik: criticism, Vernunft: reason, Gutdenkenden: well-meaning people</vocab>

<summary>Eckartshausen begins his discourse by challenging the "literary intolerance" of his time, declaring his willingness to face the harsh attacks of critics in order to speak the truth. He appeals to the spiritual presence of past scholars and the approval of "well-meaning" individuals over the insults of professional journalists.</summary>

<keywords>intellectual freedom, literary criticism, Enlightenment, Bavarian Academy of Sciences, journalism, Karl von Eckartshausen, intolerance</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Karl von Eckartshausen's 1785 speech regarding "literary intolerance." Having previously established his defiance against critics who "desecrate reason," he now turns to address the members of the Academy directly, praising their Enlightenment mission.</meta>

I speak for the cause of humanity, and this shall in every case be my defense: for what are works of the spirit <note>original: "Werke des Geistes" — referring to intellectual, creative, or philosophical output.</note> if they dishonor reason? —

You fathers of the Academy! Your praiseworthy efforts have hitherto been to spread light in the darkness, to chase away the nocturnal owls of black prejudice, and to restore the human spirit to its original rights. You taught with gentleness <term>Sanftmuth: A key Enlightenment virtue emphasizing a mild, patient, and non-violent approach to education and reform.</term>, and like the spring sun, your spirit warmed the nation and thawed the ice of ignorance. Flowers of spring sprouted in regions where once thick barrenness fettered the earth.

With manly strength, you have resisted the attacks of stupidity; gentleness and strength armed your eye, and in harmless armor you protected yourselves only against malice without persecuting it, and therefore its repeated sword-strokes glided harmlessly off your unshakable helmet.

The noble intention to benefit humanity gathered you for the first time under the protection of
->our<-

<summary>Eckartshausen praises the Bavarian Academy of Sciences for its role in the Enlightenment, using metaphors of light and nature to describe how they have overcome prejudice and ignorance through gentle but firm intellectual leadership.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, Bavarian Academy of Sciences, reason, prejudice, education, ignorance, humanity, Karl von Eckartshausen</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues Karl von Eckartshausen's 1785 speech to the Bavarian Academy of Sciences. The previous page ended with the phrase "under the protection of our," leading directly into the name of the Academy's founder, Maximilian III Joseph.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal woodcut divider with a central floral or scrollwork ornament flanked by horizontal lines.</image-desc>

---

of our unforgettable Maximilian <note>Maximilian III Joseph (1727–1777), the Elector of Bavaria who founded the Academy in 1759.</note>: philanthropic benevolence ennobled the great undertaking when men said to themselves: We wish to employ the powers of our spirit for the welfare of our fatherland. Therefore, every day that renews the memory of the founding of the Academy is a festival celebrated for the sake of humanity.

It seems to me that the guardian spirit <note>original: "Schutzgeist"</note> of our fatherland spreads its beneficial wing over us, attends this public assembly, and repeats the words that it whispered so powerfully into your hearts on the founding day of the Academy: Life is given to man for useful activity, for enjoyment, and not for dead contemplation; so it seems to me that I hear it speak. The pale night-walkers <note>A metaphor for reclusive, pedantic scholars who isolate themselves from society.</note>, who grow wild in their studies, becoming unsociable, surly, and dirty—who look always into folios <term>Folianten: Large, heavy books made from full sheets of paper, often used here to symbolize dusty, outdated, or overly academic volumes.</term> and never into nature or into themselves, who never study the world, humanity, and its passions, and are never attentive to their own deceitful hearts, nor become better and more refined themselves through the exertion of their reason—these do not deserve the name of the scholar <term>Gelehrten: A learned person or academic; Eckartshausen argues that true scholarship requires moral and social application, not just book-learning.</term>.

<summary>Eckartshausen honors the memory of Maximilian III Joseph and defines the true purpose of the Academy: to use intellectual power for the public good rather than isolated, pedantic study.</summary>
<keywords>Maximilian III Joseph, Bavarian Academy of Sciences, Enlightenment, scholarship, philanthropy, social utility, reason, fatherland</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues Karl von Eckartshausen’s 1785 speech to the Bavarian Academy of Sciences. The previous page ended with a critique of "pale night-walkers"—scholars who isolate themselves in dusty books without self-reflection or social purpose.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal ornament with a central scroll-like motif and symmetrical horizontal lines.</image-desc>

---

They are busy idlers <note>original: "geschäftige Müßiggänger"</note> in the realm of the sciences, priests of their own pride, who are forever building altars and exhibiting themselves as idols of the world. Their power of judgment is stubbornness, their learning is sophistry <term>Sophisterey: Reasoning that seems clever but is actually false or misleading, often used to deceive.</term>, their wisdom is mere solemnity <term>Solennität: Formal pomp or gravity that lacks genuine substance.</term>, their science is proud ignorance, and their zeal is an insolent addiction to erudition <note>original: "Gelehrtheitssucht"</note>.

Let not vain honor and base ambition be the reward for your academic labors: let the thought of having ever contributed something to the welfare of the whole ennoble your feelings. Neither marble nor a column of bronze <note>original: "Säule von Erz"</note> immortalizes the wise man, but rather his quiet influence on the welfare of humanity after centuries have passed. For even when the well-intentioned man is no more, when the moth has consumed his works, and through gray centuries the monument of honor <note>original: "Ehrensäule"</note> which a grateful posterity <note>original: "Nachwelt"</note> built for him has fallen: even then he lives and is great, even if no mortal knows him anymore, still active through others for the welfare of the whole, because his principles <note>original: "Grundsätze"</note> still establish good upon the earth.

<summary>Eckartshausen contrasts the "busy idleness" of vain, pedantic scholars with the true scholar's mission: to improve humanity through lasting principles rather than seeking temporary fame or monuments.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, Bavarian Academy of Sciences, scholarship, ethics, posterity, vanity, public welfare, academic virtue</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues Karl von Eckartshausen’s 1785 speech to the Bavarian Academy of Sciences. Following his critique of vain scholars on the previous page, he now shifts to a poetic and spiritual invocation, pleading for divine wisdom to guide his intellect rather than relying on cold, human reason alone.</meta>

Thus the voice of the Cherub <term>Cherub: In this context, a divine messenger or an inner spiritual voice representing higher wisdom and celestial insight.</term> echoes in my soul; a holy shudder courses through my limbs and announces to me the nothingness of human reason.

O Wisdom, daughter of heaven! Known only by sincere hearts who mean well by humanity, spread even the smallest of your rays over my head, and enlighten my understanding so that I may know you. Reveal yourself to my soul, O Divine One, as beautiful as you are, and inspire my heart so that truth may accompany my words and warmth my expression.

How well we could live here below among one another if outward behavior were always the reflection of the soul—if morality <term>Sittlichkeit: The practice of social ethics and moral conduct, which the author fears has become a mere outward performance.</term> were virtue, and virtue remained the guiding principle of our foundations! But as it is, philosophy is separated from the title of "philosopher," and wisdom from the name of the "scholar." Reason is a busily confusing thing that harbors nothing but doubt, contrives deep mysteries and then dissolves them, and—along with a frantic crowd of thinking fools—[disturbs?] those venerable...

<meta>This page continues Karl von Eckartshausen’s critique of the academic world. ...fills those madhouses that we call colleges and universities. *)

Every clumsy fool there wishes to give himself the power to fly using a magic salve, <note>Likely an allusion to "witch's salve," a legendary ointment thought to allow flight; here, it represents the shortcuts or false promises of abstract intellectualism.</note> and to penetrate the boundaries of the infinite universe: there the man often sits immersed in useless investigations, forgets himself and his duties, and makes a means into an end by always thinking, but never acting; eternally gathering principles without ever putting them into practice.

He ceases to be a helpful friend, a domestic husband, a nurturing father, a busy citizen, and even a human being, in order to become—or so he imagines—a scholar.

Thus, man lies to himself about a vocation that our nature does not recognize, and makes himself a day-laborer of the sciences. He measures his intellect with a scholastic yardstick. <note>original: "skollastischen Elle." The "ell" was a unit of measurement; Eckartshausen suggests the scholar uses rigid, outdated academic standards rather than true wisdom.</note> A piece of parchment, <note>Referring to a diploma or degree.</note> hypochondria, <note>In the 18th century, "hypochondria" was often viewed as a "scholar’s disease"—a physical and mental melancholy brought on by sedentary study and excessive overthinking.</note> a stiff body, and an inflexible soul are the proofs of his academic standing. **)

<meta>This page continues Karl von Eckartshausen’s critique of the pedantic academic world. but also, at the same time, the evidence of his unfeeling omniscience.

There are base spirits who seem created for no other purpose than to serve as a container, a register, or a warehouse for that which others have invented. They are plagiarists, translators, and hack-compilers; <note>original: "Zusammenschmieder." This literally means "those who forge things together," implying a writer who hammers together the work of others without any creative spark.</note> they do not think, but merely repeat what other authors have thought; and since the very choice of thoughts is an act of invention in itself, theirs is poor and incorrect, leading them to write a great many things rather than writing anything of excellence. *)

They possess nothing that might be considered an original <term>archetype</term> <note>original: "Urbild," meaning a prototype or a foundational, original model of thought.</note> belonging solely to them, except for their pride; and these people are often elevated to the rank of scholars by the great and by the common rabble—people whom a man of reason would scarcely count among the lowest class of humanity. **)

How true, O Pope! <note>Referring to the English poet Alexander Pope (1688–1744), who was widely admired in 18th-century Germany for his philosophical poetry.</note> is your declaration when you cried out: O you who are blinder than the blind! --- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of modern scholarship by Karl von Eckartshausen. The text picks up mid-sentence from the previous page’s discussion on the "untrodden paths" of the "blind," contrasting the arrogance of modern intellectuals with the true wisdom of the ancients.</meta>

ways, and dreams of light in tenfold darkness. Yes, it is indisputable: boast as you will, you groped yesterday and you grope still today. Reason is as dull <note>original: "dumm." In this context, it implies a lack of spiritual insight or a heavy-handed, unimaginative use of the intellect rather than literal stupidity.</note> among us as it was among the Greeks; we have our <term>Pyrrho</term> <note>Referring to Pyrrho of Elis (c. 360 – c. 270 BC), the founder of Skepticism, who argued that one must transcend the senses to find peace.</note>, our <term>Diogenes</term> <note>Diogenes of Sinope, the Cynic philosopher known for challenging social conventions and searching for an "honest man."</note>, our <term>Platos</term>—though more dull-witted than those of Greece—and we have our <term>Epicuruses</term> <note>Epicurus, whose philosophy emphasized the pursuit of tranquility and the study of the natural world.</note> as well. Everything in which Rome and Greece erred, imagined, or thought, we have it all; only we no longer have a <term>Socrates</term>. <note>Socrates is invoked here as the ideal philosopher because he prioritized ethics and the admission of his own ignorance over the creation of complex systems.</note>

It is true, there can be no greater and more worthy spectacle than to see man as he emerges from nothingness through his own powers; how he parts the darkness with which he is naturally surrounded through the light of reason; how he raises himself, as it were, above himself, wanders with his spirit through the regions of heaven, and, like the sun, traverses the immeasurable space of the universe with giant strides. Yet, far greater and more important still is the sight of man when he is seen returning into himself, in order to explore man—his nature, his duties, and his destiny <note>original: "Bestimmung," which can mean destiny, vocation, or ultimate purpose.</note>—from within himself.

<summary>The author argues that modern intellectuals repeat the errors of ancient philosophers without possessing the true wisdom of Socrates. He concludes that while exploring the cosmos is grand, the ultimate human endeavor is inward self-exploration and understanding one's destiny.</summary>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues Karl von Eckartshausen’s discourse on the human condition and the pitfalls of intellectual vanity. It follows a passage praising the potential of human reason, now pivoting to the "sad" reality of how that reason is frequently misapplied through theoretical and practical errors.</meta>

->[Image: A decorative typographic ornament featuring a central floral motif flanked by symmetrical scrolls and elegant flourishes.]<-

But it is an equally sorrowful thought when we observe how theoretical and practical errors mislead humanity, leading us into abysses.

Here, the defects of the spirit and the intellect flow into the stirrings of the soul; here, man grows accustomed to finding a seductive emotion in things that do not truly exist. In this state, he embraces the <term>chimeric</term> <note>In the 18th century, "chimeric" (from the Greek Chimera) referred to something that was a wild product of the imagination, unreal or impossible.</note> creations of his own and others' imaginations as if they were true treasures, and mistakes delusions for reliable hopes. The misguided spirit carries away his wavering taste; the <term>tasteless</term> <note>The author uses "tasteless" (*Abgeschmackte*) here not just as an aesthetic failure, but as a moral one—a lack of discernment for what is truly good and noble.</note> adopts the charms of the pleasant, and the beautiful disguises itself in the mask of the ugly. Absurd secondary notions rob excellence of its dignity and assign value to that which is worthless. Disorder and unrestraint take deep root, spreading with more power every day; they interweave themselves ever deeper into the soul’s innermost core, entrenching the dominance of excessive and destructive inclinations.

This is why the sciences, whose source is so pure and whose ultimate purpose is so laudable—

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 116, completing the word "laudable" (löblich) to describe the sciences, before contrasting their noble purpose with the corruption found in their practitioners.</meta>

[lauda]ble, can produce so many impieties, so many errors, so many absurd systems of teaching <note>The German *Lehrgebäude* refers to a "building" or structured framework of doctrine or theory.</note>, and so many contradictions: this is the reason why one finds among many of their devotees so much pride, so much greed, so much malice, so much spite, so much envy, so many lies, so many bad deeds, so many slanders, and so many low and shameful flatteries. Experience is the proof of these propositions, and, to the shame of humanity and of the Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung</term> <note>The Enlightenment was an 18th-century intellectual movement emphasizing reason and individualism over tradition.</note>, I summon as a witness the century in which we live.

As long as we still hear talk of war, says Iselin <note>Isaac Iselin (1728–1782) was a Swiss philosopher who wrote extensively on the history of humanity and social progress.</note>, we shall always be permitted to say aloud that the world is still very barbaric; and as long, I add, as we still hear of misanthropic disputes, of base scribblings <note>original: "Schreibereyen" — a derogatory term for petty or poorly written polemics.</note>, and shameful disparagements in the realm of literature, we shall always be permitted to say aloud that the world is still very unenlightened.

Ever since individuals and families renounced overt barbarism toward one another by coming together into civil societies, the states and their

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 117, completing the thought regarding how states and their rulers treat the "right of the stronger" as a divine privilege, then transitioning to a critique of the "despots of literature."</meta>

rulers have reserved the same for themselves as a precious privilege, and viewed the right of the stronger as a divine right, Iselin <note>Isaac Iselin (1728–1782), the Swiss philosopher mentioned on the previous page.</note> continues; and I add, since the gathering of scholars into societies and academies has led individuals to renounce overt stupidity toward one another, the despots of literature <note>The author uses "despots of literature" to describe influential critics or authors who use their platform to bully others and control intellectual life.</note> have nonetheless always reserved this same privilege for themselves, viewing the "right of their wit" as a divine right to disparage their fellow human beings.

It is a terrifying thought, from which a man of feeling recoils, when he reads through the multitude of pamphlets <note>original: "Broschüren" — in this context, refers to cheap, sensationalist, or polemical publications rather than informational booklets.</note> that have for some time flooded the world; these are works of neither the spirit nor the heart, but are full of vulgar expressions, artful slander, and abhorrent misanthropy <term>Menschenfeindlichkeit</term> <note>Literally "hatred of humanity"; a deep-seated distrust or dislike of the human race.</note>. They place their strength in wit and satirical expression and, instead of uplifting humanity, they degrade it in the most shameful manner. Such writings are spongy growths of the heart; they are precursors to cancerous hardenings that cause incurable evils.

<summary>The author extends Isaac Iselin’s critique of political tyranny to the intellectual world, condemning "literary despots" who use their wit to attack others. --- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the "despots of literature," shifting focus to the concept of tolerance and the hypocrisy of certain Enlightenment thinkers who mirror the cruelty of the religious zealots they claim to oppose.</meta>

O tolerance! Tolerance! You are such a glorious word, written so often, and yet felt so little in our century. Just as once the fanatic with a distorted face, out of false religious zeal, dragged people by their hair to the stake <note>original: "Scheiterhaufen" — the pyre used for execution by burning.</note>, so some of our Enlighteners <term>Aufklärer</term> <note>The "Aufklärer" were proponents of the Enlightenment, a movement valuing reason; here, the author accuses them of being just as dogmatic as their predecessors.</note> in their writings tear at the honor of their fellow brothers through misanthropic insults, leading them to the <term>auto-da-fé</term> <note>From the Portuguese "act of faith"; the public ritual of penance for heretics, often followed by execution.</note> of their wit *). — Believe! they once cried, or you will be condemned to the stake: think like us! our false philosophers now cry, or we will brand you in our writings and whip you through half the world. So cry the executioners of literature, and unashamedly nail the name of the most honest man to the gallows of their journals.

The man who can persecute a person, who hates social classes, and out of a tendency toward lear— <note>The sentence breaks off here and continues on the following page.</note>

De la Rochefoucauld. <note>François de La Rochefoucauld (1613–1680), a French author famous for his cynical and pithy maxims.</note>

The truest mark of being born with great qualities is to be born without envy. <note>original French: "La plus veritable marque d'être né avec de grandes qualités, c'est, d'être né sans envie."</note>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 119, completing the sentence describing those who claim to be "Enlighteners" but act with the same cruelty as the religious zealots they despise. The previous page ended with the fragmented word "lear—" (ge=lehrten).</meta>

You! whoever you are, who sees with a suffering heart the disorders that afflict humanity, and who would like to set them right: write about prejudices, fight vice, but never insult the individual. — A man who insults people, even if he possessed Rousseau's spirit <note>Jean-Jacques Rousseau (1712–1778), a Swiss-French philosopher whose ideas influenced the Enlightenment and the French Revolution.</note> and Voltaire's expression <note>Voltaire (1694–1778) was famous for his sharp wit and advocacy for civil liberties.</note>, is a monster in my eyes.

> *) He who, in writing, has regard only for the taste of his century, thinks more of his own person than of his writings. One must always strive for perfection, and then that justice, which is sometimes denied us by our contemporaries, posterity will know how to return to us.

Oh! We would be happier. Tolerance is constantly preached in our century, and yet no one is more intolerant than the scholars who preach it. They avenge the slightest insult with public <term>Pasquils</term> <note>original: "Pasquillen"; satirical, often anonymous lampoons or pamphlets used to publicly shame or mock an opponent</note>. —

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the "learned" men and journalists of the 18th century. Following the previous page's discussion on how intellectual refinement can often mask a deeper malice, this page focuses specifically on the role of the press and the abuse of public reputation.</meta>

It is well known how unjust many journalists of our century are. They accept data sent in by anyone at all under anonymous names, and commit alleged actions to public print, including the first and last names of those who are supposed to have performed them—all under the title of <term>Publicity</term> <note>original: "Publizität"; in this context, the author is critiquing the Enlightenment principle that all information should be shared publicly, arguing it is used as a shield for character assassination.</note>. Many a man has experienced how much humanity suffers under this error, finding his reputation and honorable name branded for centuries in such journals. It is extremely unjust, and the highest insult to humanity, to expose even the weaknesses of men—I will not even say their vices—and to do so moreover amidst the greatest uncertainty. Every insidious and slanderous villain dares to invent actions for an honest man, spawned from his own evil heart, and then the honest man stands upon the <term>Pillory</term> <note>original: "Pranger"; a device for public punishment consisting of a wooden frame with holes for the head and hands, intended to expose the offender to public scorn.</note> of the world. 

Once he has already been publicly presented, only then do the gentlemen journalists allow him to defend himself. That is like conducting a trial for someone once his head has already been severed from his body. A slander spoken within a circle of six friends dishonors; but communicated to the whole world in five thousand printed copies—it dishonors

His parrot-like art is welcomed by many a bookseller, as long as his chatter is new and brings in money. Thus, those shameful, despotic, and selfish printing-peddlers <term>Druckkrämer</term> <note>A derogatory term for publishers or booksellers who prioritize profit over the intellectual or moral quality of the literature they produce.</note> of our century dishonor humanity; thus some fatten themselves at the expense of the human spirit. <meta>This page continues the author's polemic against the degradation of literature and the publishing industry. The previous page concluded by lamenting how great works of philosophy and theology are placed alongside scandalous "lampoons" in bookshops, driven solely by profit.</meta>

O let me forget this abomination! Reason <term>Vernunft</term> <note>In the Enlightenment context, "Vernunft" refers to the human capacity to understand the world through logic and evidence, independent of dogma.</note> has become a public harlot who sells herself to the highest bidder; and many a bookseller offers his wretched midwifery services to the bastards of human unreason, yet still counts himself among the men of the Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung</term>.

Such, alas, is the state of the image of our Enlightenment era, and such remnants of barbarism <term>Barbarey</term> still disfigure the regions where the sciences <term>Wissenschaften</term> <note>In the 18th century, "Wissenschaften" referred to the broad spectrum of systematic scholarship and academic disciplines, not just the natural sciences.</note> dwell.

The scholar <term>Gelehrte</term>, whose sublime calling required him to preserve the sacred rights of humanity <term>Menschheit</term> inviolate, and to spread the exalted principles of the Good and the True in the purest clarity among men—his brothers—now abandons the noble paths of reason and becomes a literary persecutor.

It is true, our happy times possess many virtuous, truly enlightened philanthropists <term>Menschenfreunde</term> <note>Literally "friends of humanity," a key ideal of the era emphasizing universal benevolence and social progress.</note>; yet how small is their number compared to the enormous swarm of those who prove themselves unworthy of the name of a scholar and philoso—

There is an old saying: one begins to become learned when one begins to realize that one knows nothing <note>A reference to the Socratic paradox, "I know that I know nothing."</note>; yet it seems that this truth has been forgotten once again: nowadays, everyone wants to know everything. Works of the mind <term>Geistes</term> <note>The German *Geist* encompasses mind, spirit, and intellect; here it refers to intellectual or literary productions.</note>, if they are to be of use, are flowers that bloom only under the mild and refreshing shade of true modesty and humility, and wither and die in the midday heat of pride. Real merits might perhaps protect a haughty person from our contempt, but never from the secret hatred of the human heart: for what heart does not possess a hidden inclination toward pride, which may indeed be mastered by virtue, but can never be entirely uprooted? Will this inclination not awaken when it is insulted and offended by the injustice of another’s pride? Only humility <term>Demuth</term> and modesty are the true conquerors of hearts. No soul—unless a base and malicious envy is its ruling passion—will resist the impression of modesty, provided it does not force our esteem upon itself; if it rather leaves us the merit <note>In this context, *Verdienst* refers to the internal sense of satisfaction or "credit" one feels for being fair-minded.</note> of believing that we are being just toward it, without compelling us to be just; if it does not take away the power [to...]

Criticism, if it is to be noble, must have as its purpose the improvement of the one at whom it is directed; if this is not your motive, then your heart is corrupt and there is malice in your soul. <meta>This page continues the argument against harsh, public criticism, specifically addressing how such insults prevent the target from self-improvement and instead encourage social malice.</meta>

A person may speak whatever he wants, and as poorly as he wants; he will always find his people who enjoy listening to him and soon begin to parrot <note>Original: *nachlallen*; literally to babble or stammer after someone, suggesting mindless imitation.</note> him. <meta>This page continues the argument regarding the ethical way to provide criticism, specifically focusing on how the critic's tone affects the recipient’s willingness to reform or their descent into defensiveness.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<sig>K</sig>

-><image-desc>A horizontal typographic ornament consisting of a central stylized floral element flanked by double lines and scrolls.</image-desc><-

...since even the mere observation of a faulty side is humiliating enough for him in itself. When coarseness <term>Grobheit</term> and impetuosity <term>Ungestümm</term> are the language of the censurer, the person being criticized loses the entire true perspective at once; he then no longer looks at the mistake he committed, but at the current insult and the wounding <term>Kränkung</term> of his honor <term>Ehre</term> <note>In this period, "honor" was considered a vital social and moral asset; to damage it publicly was seen as a profound injury to a person’s civic existence.</note>. He sees nothing more in the critic <term>Kritiker</term> than an enemy, and the coarseness with which he is treated—along with his perception of the shame being brought upon him before others—prevents him from thinking any further about the mistake he made; instead, it stirs up his heart to resistance <term>Widerseßlichkeit</term> and a desire for revenge <term>Rachbegierde</term>. Every further word brought against him is pure insult. He no longer feels anything else, for he expects nothing good from his enemy, but only the intention to harm him.

Conversely, if the critic spares the tender feelings he has for his neighbor's honor in every possible way; if gentleness <term>Sanftmuth</term> accompanies the words that rebuke the error; if no proud elevation over his person, no drive to find fault and find spots in him, no thirst for revenge <term>Rachsucht</term> to hurt or offend him, nor any other ignoble passion accompanies our pen; if true, up- <note>The text breaks off mid-word; likely "aufrichtige," meaning sincere or upright.</note>

<summary>This page argues that harsh, public criticism causes the recipient to focus on their wounded pride rather than their mistake, leading to resentment instead of improvement. It advocates for gentleness and a respect for the "neighbor's honor" as the only effective way to offer correction.</summary>

<keywords>ethics of criticism, honor, gentleness, psychological resistance, social conduct, rhetoric of correction</keywords>

# Appendix
## to my Speech on Literary Intolerance. <note>"Literary intolerance" (litterarische Intoleranz) refers to the professional animosity, jealousy, and public feuding common among scholars in the 18th-century "Republic of Letters."</note>

---

In the *Nuremberg Scholarly Journal*, issue 39 of this year, my speech was reviewed. I include the review here as an appendix to my speech, because it is the clearest proof and confirmation of the truth of my propositions.

The Mr. Reviewer first endeavors to give the matter a completely false twist, claiming: that I understand "literary intolerance" to mean when one wishes to see further than the short-sighted, or when one wishes to believe less than tradition <note>The German *Herkommen* refers to established customs or long-standing religious traditions.</note> commands one to believe.

How false this attribution is, is shown by my speech; for by literary intolerance I mean the degenerate incompatibility of scholars, who constantly disparage one another in their writings in the most abominable manner. See pages 7, 8, 9.

<meta>catchword: If</meta>

<vocab>Appendix, literary intolerance, Nuremberg Scholarly Journal, review, confirmation, the short-sighted, tradition, incompatibility of scholars</vocab>

<summary>The author begins an appendix to his speech by responding to a critique in a Nuremberg journal, arguing that the reviewer misinterpreted his definition of "literary intolerance." He clarifies that he is not criticizing intellectual progress, but rather the toxic behavior and mutual disparagement practiced by scholars.</summary>
<keywords>literary intolerance, Nuremberg Scholarly Journal, book review, scholarly feuds, tradition, 18th-century academia</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's rebuttal against a critic from the Nuremberg Scholarly Journal. The previous page established that the reviewer misrepresented the author's definition of "literary intolerance" as a defense of blind tradition. Here, the author clarifies that he supports enlightened progress and the dismantling of prejudice, provided it is done without personal malice.</meta>

-> <image-desc>decorative horizontal printer's ornament with a central circular rosette between horizontal lines</image-desc> <-

When I say, and wish on page 36, that man, led by the bond of love, should seek virtue in his heart and wisdom in his soul, and should not value the quiet joys of *religion* less than the principles of an evil *philosophy*, that certainly does not mean blaming someone if they believe less than tradition <note>original: "Herkommen"; refers to established social and religious customs</note> commands.

The author of the *Critique* <note>referring to the review of his work</note> also presumes of me that I understand *intolerance* to mean tearing the mask from the face of ignorance and exposing prejudices in their nakedness.

The speech itself shows how false this attribution is. All my works also contradict the *reviewer*. I myself wrote against prejudices and unmasked them; but I spared the person.

On page 13 it also says: 
> You, whoever you may be, who with a suffering heart sees the disorders that afflict humanity, and who would like to make them right—write about prejudices, fight vice, but never insult the person etc. <note>The symbol "ꝛc." is an archaic abbreviation for "etcetera."</note>

<vocab>Religion, Philosophy, Critique, Intolerance, Prejudices, Humanity, Reviewer, Vice</vocab>

<summary>The author clarifies that his opposition to "literary intolerance" is not a defense of ignorance, but a plea for civility; he argues that one can combat prejudice and vice without attacking the dignity of the individual.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, religious tolerance, polemics, ethics in writing, personal conduct, Nuremberg Scholarly Journal, critique of philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense against a negative review. He distinguishes between criticizing societal vices and attacking individuals, defending his use of "publicity" while condemning its abuse as character assassination. He accuses his critics of superficial reading and filling pages without understanding the content.</meta>

I am also no enemy of **publicity** <note>original: "Publizität"; the practice of making ideas and information public through printing and discourse</note>, but rather of the **abuse of publicity**. See page 17. It is said that in public writings, the most upright man is slandered, and unknown faults are discovered in him under the title of publicity. — Which human principles allow this? — To me, critiques are also not **executioner’s business** <note>original: "Henkersgeschäfte"; a metaphor for criticism that aims to destroy a person's reputation or "execute" their character rather than improve their work</note>; see page 31, where I describe the critic as he should be.

Moreover, where is there intolerance in my speech? — That I speak about vice and about abuses does not mean being intolerant. One need only show me a single line in all my writings where I have insulted a person — persecuting people and persecuting vice — are, after all, very different. I am attaching this critique as a **supplement** <note>original: "Beylage"</note> to my speech, and it proves clearly that the gentlemen **reviewers** <note>original: "Rezensenten"</note> review without reading, and write things haphazardly <note>original: "in den Tag hinein"; an idiom meaning thoughtlessly or just to pass the time</note> just to fill the page.

Since I have thought deeply about the matter and am convinced of the truth, such reviews—which do not at all bear the stamp <note>The text cuts off here at "Geprä-", likely for "Gepräge," meaning the character or quality of a proper critique.</note>—do not move me in the slightest to change my opinion.

<vocab>Publicity, Reviewers, Intolerance, Critique, Supplement, Abuse</vocab>

ge

<summary>The author defends his work against critics, arguing that attacking societal vices is not the same as personal intolerance. He maintains that while he supports the public exchange of ideas, he opposes using the press for character assassination.</summary>

<keywords>publicity, literary criticism, intolerance, vice, slander, Enlightenment, reviewers, character assassination</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's rebuttal of a specific critique. On the previous page, he argued that critics often write without reading and that their reviews lack the proper character or "stamp" of genuine scholarship.</meta>

of a review, and are completely unworthy of a scholar <note>original: "Gelehrten"</note>, who should never maliciously twist the matter.

I would be sorry, however, if this supplement <note>original: "Beylage"; likely referring to the printed critique he has attached to his own work for the reader to see</note> could ever lead a reviewer to suspect that it caused me even a single moment of annoyance. I value a sensible review as much as I despise an ungrounded one. I do not write for the applause of the reviewer, but for the approval of the reasonable man; what do I care for the remaining swarm of the humming gnats of literature <note>original: "sumsenden Schnaken der Litteratur"; a dismissive metaphor for petty critics who annoy authors without providing substance</note>, who use their wit for nothing but to pester people and nourish themselves with their stingers. My heart and my intention defend me when I write; therefore, the outcome does not worry me.

Should someone recently—as I have no doubt they will—twist some passages in this piece as well, they can be assured that I will not respond to it at all; for I would not even have rebuked this review if I had not just been in the mood to cite an example to prove how few reviewers are what they ought to be.

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, where the author dismissed petty critics as "humming gnats," he now transitions to a broader critique of the contemporary media landscape and the proliferation of periodical literature.</meta>

## Now a few more words about the mania for journalism <note>original: "Journalisterey"; the suffix "-ey" often added a pejorative tone, suggesting a obsessive or debased practice, akin to "journal-mongering."</note> and public discourse.

The addiction to writing, reading, and subscribing to journals has become so universal these days that it seems worth the effort and necessary for me to cast an attentive eye upon it.

In the days when taste and the various branches of knowledge <note>original: "Wissenschaften"; in this era, the term referred to all scholarly learning and "sciences" in the broadest sense.</note> were first blossoming, journals occupied themselves with making the charms of these subjects generally known. They did not yet drown the public in a flood of periodical writings; rather, their intention was only to awaken the zeal of their readers and to show the heart how useful knowledge could be. To this end, journals or periodical writings performed very beneficial services. They included essays on many different subjects and excerpts from various good books within their pages, thereby spreading a general desire for what is good and beautiful, and the urge to drink from the source itself. How noble were the writings of *The Northern Overseer* <note>original: "nordischer Aufseher"; an influential moral weekly published in Copenhagen (1758–1761) edited by J.A. Schlegel.</note>, *The Youth* <note>original: "Der Jüngling"; a mid-18th-century moral journal.</note>, the *English Spectator* <note>original: "englischer Zuschauer"; referring to the famous London periodical by Addison and Steele which inspired the "Moral Weekly" genre across Europe.</note>, and the like, which distinguished themselves particularly through pure morality.

<term>Journalisterey</term> — A dismissive term for the excessive production and consumption of magazines and newspapers.
<term>Publizität</term> — Public life or the state of being public; here referring to the emerging "public sphere" of shared information.
<term>Periodische Schriften</term> — Periodical writings; publications issued at regular intervals, like magazines.
<term>Moralität</term> — Morality; specifically the 18th-century focus on using literature to improve the character and ethics of the reader.

<summary>The author reflects on the rise of journalism, contrasting the beneficial "moral weeklies" of the past—which inspired a love for learning—with what he sees as a modern, overwhelming addiction to periodical literature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the misuse of publicity, following directly from the previous page's incomplete sentence: "...it is human—"</meta>

insult; it is lampoonery <note>original: "Pasquillmacherey"; refers to the writing of anonymous, satirical, and often libelous attacks against individuals.</note>. ->I shall prove it.<-

Every state promises its citizens the security of their honor, an assurance that establishes the condition in which the citizen has nothing to fear regarding their reputation.

Honor is the respect for a citizen’s integrity, and this respect is linked to civic consequences. Whatever deprives a citizen of this respect robs them of actual advantages and causes them true harm.

In the common understanding, both a physical and a verbal insult is regarded as an attack on our honor, and is therefore an offense.

The police <term>Polizey: In the 18th century, this term encompassed the general administration, public order, and the maintenance of communal welfare, rather than just a modern law enforcement body.</term> must both secure that respect for the citizen which carries civic consequences, and defend them against any physical or verbal insult.

| **Menschheit** | Humanity; the collective dignity of the human race. |
| **Pöbel** | The rabble or mob; those perceived to lack discernment. |
| **Ruhe der Staaten** | The peace/tranquility of states; social and political stability. |
| **Mitbürger** | Fellow citizen; an individual sharing in the social contract. |
| **Sicherheit der Ehre** | Security of honor; the legal protection of one's reputation. |
| **Rechtschaffenheit** | Integrity or uprightness; the quality of being honest and morally right. |
| **Bürgerliche Folgen** | Civic consequences; the practical social or legal effects of one's status. |
| **Schmähung** | Abuse or vituperation; degrading language or actions. |
| **Polizey** | Public administration; the governance of public order and safety. |

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal protection of honor, building on the definition of honor as a citizen's integrity and the state's duty to defend it.</meta>

Insults which, through words or deeds, attack the honor of citizens—even if they entail no legal consequences <note>The author refers to "bürgerliche Folgen" (civic consequences), meaning insults that might not result in a formal loss of rights or status but still damage one's standing.</note>—are nevertheless, at least on the part of the insulter, true offenses against which the police laws <term>Polizeygesetze: In this period, "police" referred to the broad administrative governance of a state's internal order, welfare, and security.</term> must provide protection. Verbal insults are either oral or written; physical insults <note>original: "thätige Beschimpfungen"; insults through actions or "insults by deed," such as a slap or a mocking gesture, intended to humiliate.</note> are those which occur to the shame of another, whether directed at their own person or their relatives.

Oral insults are to be prohibited through police regulations, for they are a prelude to greater disorders, and such attacks on a good reputation often have the most harmful influence on the welfare of the citizen *).

Since these are the true principles of a well-ordered police system, it is quite easy to conclude what is to be thought of the publicity <note>Here, "publicity" refers to the public nature of the press and the mass circulation of information.</note> of our journalism <note>original: "Journalisterey"; a dismissive or pejorative term for the press of the time.</note>. I declare it to be a public violation of the law of nations <term>Völkerrecht: The body of rules governing the interactions between different states and the rights of their subjects abroad.</term>. It disturbs the order of the state; for since foreign journalists publicly insult the honor of the citizen and

and insult them, the domestic laws are too weak to protect the citizen against foreign insults.

The assassins of honor entrench themselves abroad, and the state cannot save the honor of its own people from these hidden backstabbers <note>original: "Meuchelmörder"; while literally meaning "assassins," in this context it refers to those who "murder" a person's reputation from the safety of a foreign jurisdiction.</note>.

Just as a foreign state cannot, without violating the law of nations <term>Völkerrecht: The historical term for International Law, governing the conduct between sovereign states.</term>, protect a murderer who daily jeopardizes the lives of the citizens of a neighboring state, so too can a state not tolerate libelous <term>Pasquillanischen: Derived from 'pasquinade,' a type of biting, anonymous lampoon or satirical libel common in the 18th century.</term> journalists without violating the law of nations. Reverence for the Prince, respect for the heads of the legislature, and precise obedience maintain order among the citizens. Whoever overthrows or weakens these is an instigator of sedition <note>original: "Aufwiegler"; someone who stirs up social or political unrest.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limits of "publicity" (public discourse and the freedom of the press). The text picks up from the previous page's final sentence, completing the thought that while public debate exposes government flaws, it must be handled with caution and expertise.</meta>

...made us aware of many flaws in the state constitution <term>Staatsverfassung: The fundamental legal and political framework governing a state.</term> and of many oppressions <term>Bedrückungen: The hardships or injustices suffered by the common people under authority.</term> of humanity. However, everything has its limits. Publicity <term>Publizität: The practice of making government actions and political debate public; the "public sphere."</term> also has its own limits, and it remains venerable only so long as it stays within them.

It is true that one has the right to speak about everything that concerns one's own well-being and woe, or the well-being and woe of the nation; but modesty <term>Bescheidenheit: Here meaning intellectual humility and restraint in judgment.</term>, thoroughness <term>Gründlichkeit: The quality of being deep and exhaustive in one's research or thinking.</term>, and love for humanity <term>Menschenliebe: An Enlightenment ideal of benevolence and care for the welfare of others.</term> must guide the pen of the friend of truth. One must always distinguish whether one intends to speak about the whole and about general principles of politics, or to highlight specific facts as they relate to the whole. One certainly has the right to speak; but in the proper place, and with the proper insights.

How often a matter seems to have very ill consequences for the state and the private interest of some individual; but it *only seems* to be so, and precisely because one does not perceive the causes that prompted this decree <note>original: "Verfügung"; an administrative order or official regulation.</note>, the circumstances under which it occurred, and the distant good ultimate purpose <term>Endzweck: The final goal or teleological aim of a policy or action.</term>, which is visible only to the eye of the one who worked on the matter. Often, its effect is only revealed when the immodest judgments of the matters have long been forgotten.

<summary>The author argues that while public criticism is necessary to expose government flaws, it requires expertise and restraint. He cautions that many state actions only appear harmful to those who lack the insider knowledge of their long-term administrative goals.</summary>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the superficiality caused by contemporary media. While the previous page focused on the social damage and loss of reputation caused by journals, this page shifts the focus to the intellectual consequences, specifically the decline of genuine scholarship in favor of shallow, performative knowledge. The catchword "wahre" (true) suggests the next page will begin by defining "true" learning or values.</meta>

Every well-meaning person recognizes the consequences of this, and we experience them every day.

Scholarship <term>Gelehrsamkeit: In the 18th century, this referred to "erudition" or deep, systematic learning across many fields, rather than just specialized schooling.</term> does not consist in being able to name a great many book titles, tell all sorts of anecdotes, or provide half-spoken and half-thought information about everything in the company of ignorant or timid people. Rather, it consists in thoroughly understanding—from the very foundation <note>original: "aus dem Grunde"; meaning fundamentally or from first principles.</note>—that which belongs to one’s professional disciplines <term>Berufswissenschaften: The specific fields of study required for one's career or vocation, such as law, medicine, or theology.</term>, to the general dignity of man, and to other necessary knowledge or favorite sciences. It means understanding these subjects in all their parts and variations, under all circumstances, and with the great ultimate purpose of being useful to humanity.

Yet at present, this last-described and solely useful scholarship is becoming rarer and less respected day by day. People find it far easier to help themselves by using various journals, libraries, and reviews <term>Rezensionen: Book reviews or critical summaries in periodicals which, while informative, were often blamed for allowing readers to pretend they had read the full works.</term>, gathering a bit here and there and everywhere. They think that if they can reap applause with such scraps, muddle through every situation, and even gain patronage <term>Protektionen: Influence or favor from powerful social or political figures, often necessary for career advancement at the time.</term>, it is not necessary to learn anything solid. --- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of the royal Prussian cabinet decree mentioned on the previous page. It outlines the legal principles used to justify the censorship of Gökingk’s journal, specifically focusing on the restrictions placed on "private individuals" when discussing matters of state.</meta>

<page-num>179</page-num>
<sig>M 2</sig>

<image-desc>A decorative printer's ornament featuring a central shell or floral motif with horizontal lines extending to both sides.</image-desc>

A private individual is not entitled to pass public, even critical judgments regarding the actions, proceedings, laws, measures, and orders of sovereigns and courts, their civil servants, administrative bodies <term>Kollegien; boards of officials who governed specific branches of administration</term>, and courts of law. Nor are they permitted to make known or distribute through print any reports concerning these matters that may come to them. A private individual is also completely incapable of making such judgments, as they lack full knowledge of the circumstances and motives.

Such a person exposes themselves to the danger of spreading slanders and untruths, and of making themselves just as complicit in them—and thus just as punishable for them—as the original author is himself. In such a case, they also cannot, according to the law, avoid naming the author and the person who submitted the material; nor can they, through their own presentation of the matter, escape the punishment <term>Ahndung; a formal legal retribution or penalty</term> resulting from this disclosure.

<note>This passage reflects the absolutist view of the era, where "reason of state" was considered beyond the comprehension or jurisdiction of ordinary citizens.</note>

**Vocabulary:**
<term>Privatperson</term> (Private individual), <term>Souverainen</term> (Sovereigns), <term>Staatsbedienten</term> (Civil servants), <term>Gerichtshöfe</term> (Courts of law), <term>Beurtheilung</term> (Judgment/Assessment), <term>Verleumdungen</term> (Slanders), <term>Urheber</term> (Author/Originator), <term>Ahndung</term> (Legal punishment/retribution).

The nation is not enlightened or improved by such reckless publications, but rather corrupted. The reckless mania for enlightenment <note>original: *Aufklärungssucht*; a derogatory term used by critics of the Enlightenment to describe what they saw as an obsessive or harmful desire to challenge traditional authorities</note> of current times is degenerating into an insolent licentiousness <note>original: *Ausgelassenheit*; here meaning a lack of moral or legal restraint</note>, tramples everything holy and venerable underfoot, makes everything contemptible to the people, confuses their concepts, and instills in them rebellion, disobedience, lack of restraint, and principles of resistance, without instructing, enlightening, or improving them.

The licentiousness of some journalists, collectors, etc., causes unpleasantness with other sovereign courts, and it is high time and a matter of necessity to put a rein on them.

It is therefore incumbent upon Mr. Gökingk <note>Leopold Friedrich Günther von Goeckingk (1748–1828), a well-known poet and Prussian official who edited the *Journal von und für Deutschland*</note> to immediately submit his excuses, grounds for defense, and proposals for a future and unobjectionable arrangement to the Halberstadt government, or face unpleasant measures; he cannot be exempted from this.

->Berlin, the 13th of December 1784.<-

->To the Government at Halberstadt.<-

->Finkenstein, Herzberg.<-
<note>Karl Wilhelm Finck von Finckenstein and Ewald Friedrich von Hertzberg were leading cabinet ministers under Frederick the Great; their signatures gave the decree the weight of royal authority.</note>

## My Dear Sir!

I read your *Paragraphs to Bavaria’s Preachers*; I cannot hide from you that I found much within them to be extremely striking, and I must write down my thoughts regarding this to you.

If I knew who you were, I would go to your study <note>original: *Stube*; literally 'room', but here implying a private office or living space</note> and say to you: Friend! we are Bavarians who love sincerity and our fatherland. The nation is not enlightened or improved by such thoughtless announcements, but rather corrupted. The reckless mania for enlightenment <term>Aufklärungssucht: literally an "addiction" or "mania" for Enlightenment; a derogatory term used by critics to describe the spread of radical or superficial ideas</term> of current times degenerates into a brazen insolence, tramples underfoot everything that is holy and venerable, makes everything contemptible to the people, confuses their concepts, and instills in them riotousness, disobedience, licentiousness, and principles of resistance, without educating, enlightening, or improving them.

The insolence <term>Ausgelassenheit: a lack of restraint or "unbridled" behavior</term> of some journalists, collectors, and so on, causes unpleasantness with other courts <note>The Prussian government was concerned that local journalists might offend foreign rulers, leading to diplomatic incidents.</note>, and it is high time and a necessity to place a bridle upon it.

It is therefore the duty of Mr. Gökingk <note>Leopold Friedrich Günther von Goeckingk (1748–1828), a well-known poet and Prussian official who edited the "Journal von und für Deutschland."</note> to immediately—and under threat of unpleasant measures from the Halberstadt government regarding this journal in general—submit his apologies, grounds for defense, and proposals for a future and unobjectionable arrangement; he cannot be exempted from this.

->To the government at Halberstadt.<-

The nation is not enlightened or improved by such thoughtless announcements, but rather corrupted. The thoughtless mania for enlightenment <note>original: "Aufklärungssucht"—a pejorative term used by critics of the Enlightenment to describe what they saw as a reckless or obsessive pursuit of reason that undermined traditional authority</note> of current times is degenerating into a brazen insolence; it tramples underfoot everything that is sacred and venerable, makes everything contemptible to the people, confuses their concepts, and instills in them rebellion, disobedience, licentiousness, and principles of resistance, without instructing, enlightening, or improving them.

The insolence of certain journalists, compilers, etc., causes unpleasantness with other courts <note>meaning the royal courts of other European states</note>, and it is of the highest time and necessity to put a bridle on it.

The text begins mid-sentence, following the catchword "bin" (am/if I am) from the preceding page, as the author establishes a tone of patriotic and civil disagreement.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative typographical ornament featuring symmetrical scrolled foliage and a central floral motif.</image-desc>

if I am of an erroneous opinion, I will gladly let myself be taught; but if you find that I am right here and there, then you will also not take it ill of me; for we wish to discuss the matter with one another in no unpleasant tone.

Nothing is easier than to make one’s neighbor look ridiculous; one needs no intellect for this, only wit and a bit of an ill-natured heart. You are a man without prejudice, and I hope you shall find me to be the same. Love for the fatherland was undoubtedly your motivation for writing, and this is also mine.

It seems to me that one should not repeat things that have been said unskillfully: often the length of time has fortunately erased the impressions of absurd things from the memory of the common folk <term>Pöbel: a term used here for the uneducated masses or the general public; the author implies that repeating errors to a reading public is worse than the errors themselves</term>; and is it truly a fine thing to renew them?

See here, my friend! I now actually assume that all the absurdities you cite were indeed spoken from the pulpits, yet perhaps only a few hundred who were at the sermon heard them, and have since forgotten the matter.

<vocab>erroneous opinion, prejudice, fatherland, memory, common folk, pulpit, sermon</vocab>

<summary>The author appeals for a civil and patriotic dialogue, arguing that publicly mocking the "absurdities" of local preachers is counterproductive because it preserves errors that the public would have otherwise forgotten. This page highlights the 18th-century debate over the "public sphere" and whether the flaws of the Church should be aired in print.</summary>

forgotten. <note>The page begins with the fragment "geßen," completing the word "vergessen" (forgotten) from the previous page.</note> But now you repeat all these absurdities in public print, and that which previously only hundreds knew, thousands know now; is that called being just toward the public? Believe me, my friend! I would have found it very commendable if you had spoken generally of the abuses that so often creep into spiritual discourses, without citing the conspicuous examples. It is always a good intention when one seeks to instruct, when one wants to eradicate errors: but this must happen with gentleness, with mildness, and with philanthropic indulgence <term>menschenfreundlicher Nachsicht: a key Enlightenment-era value suggesting a kind, patient tolerance toward the failings of others</term>—character traits that are so becoming of a noble heart. Wanting to improve others must not become a passion; if one wishes to lead one's brother away from wrong paths, one must reach out a hand to him, and not insult him with abusive words.

Dear friend! Nature has not given the advantages of the mind to everyone, and not everyone had the fortune of a good education; but regardless of this, there can still be men who, without education, without the adornment of their minds, have good souls.

I knew men who, with simplicity, presented the truths of religion to their parishioners.

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of uneducated but well-meaning country preachers. On the previous page, the author noted that they knew men who presented religious truths with simplicity; here, the author admits their rhetorical flaws but argues that private correction would have been more "brotherly" than public ridicule.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative typographical ornament featuring a central symmetrical flourish flanked by double horizontal lines.</image-desc>

It is true, their speeches were not orderly, their expressions were flawed, but what they said was nonetheless good. Do you not believe that if you had gone to such a man, if you had said to him with brotherly love: "Good father! You are an honest man, but your delivery is striking, this turn of the matter is improper, the parables are not good"; do you not believe that such a man, with warm heartfelt feeling, would have thanked you for your friendly advice?

My worthy friend! A man who presents the truths of religion is always an important man, and if there are some who have faults, one must never make them ridiculous before the public. The common people <term>Pöbel: a term used here to describe the uneducated masses who, in the eyes of Enlightenment thinkers, lacked the "reason" to process complex social critiques</term> do not have enough reason to distinguish, and they are happy when given material to laugh at those who are supposed to instruct them in the duties of Christianity: they do not distinguish the true from the untrue, and easily, like a spirited horse without a bridle, they rid themselves of religion, which has long seemed a burden to their wild hearts.

<vocab>Religion, Christianity, Common People, Brotherly Love, Advice, Reason</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that publicly mocking the rhetorical mistakes of simple preachers undermines the social and moral authority of religion. He suggests that private, "brotherly" correction is the only virtuous way to address the flaws of well-intentioned but uneducated clergy.</summary>
<keywords>clerical education, religious satire, public order, brotherly love, Enlightenment morality, social stability</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of a writer who has publicly ridiculed simple country preachers. The author argues that the writer's harsh, insulting tone is counterproductive and hypocritical, noting that true correction should be done with kindness rather than public shaming.</meta>

My friend! Your intention was to be useful, was it not? But why did you present matters in such a disagreeable way? Reproaches embitter the spirit <term>Gemüth: an 18th-century German term referring to the seat of a person's emotions, character, and soul</term>; they do not improve it. It is something so noble to view people from their good side as well, to instruct them of their mistakes with philanthropic indulgence, and to remain amiable even when pointing out their errors. A moderate zeal for truth proves the sincerity of our good intentions; insults <term>Schmähungen: bitter, abusive language or vituperation intended to degrade someone</term> betray passion and outrage hearts; why then this bitter tone in your discourse? Why the exaggeration of the smallest matters in such a hostile way, and why a public shaming of various men who are, after all, also your fellow humans and fellow citizens? I must reproach you here: you wrote about such an important subject, which every man to whom the pulpit <term>Kanzel: the raised platform in a church from which a preacher delivers a sermon</term> is entrusted should take deeply to heart, and yet you clothe your material in a degrading style of writing.

You rail against fanaticism, and you yourself become a fanatic in your writing. —

You accuse many a clergyman of intolerance, and your expressions, with which you [describe?] them... <note>original: "be="; this is a catchword, likely leading into "beschreiben" (describe) or "behandeln" (treat) on the following page.</note>

<summary>The author rebukes his contemporary for using a harsh, insulting tone when critiquing the clergy, arguing that public shaming is a "fanatical" and "intolerant" way to promote the truth. He maintains that moral correction must be handled with kindness and respect for the dignity of the person being corrected.</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, fanaticism, intolerance, pulpit, Gemüth, public shaming, brotherly love, Enlightenment critique</keywords>

My dear Sir! All the good contained in your little work is ruined by such expressions. A reasonable person thinks: this man can scold and insult, but he cannot improve anything; for he must first cultivate his own heart if he wishes to cultivate others <note>The author uses the word "bilden," a central Enlightenment concept referring to the moral and intellectual formation of a person.</note>.

Many of the stories, too, my friend! which you cite in order to make many a preacher look ridiculous, are mere fabrications—for example, the little anecdote you attributed to the good Father Franz Paul in Dorfen <note>Dorfen is a market town in Upper Bavaria; Father Franz Paul was likely a local cleric known to the readers of the time.</note>; I already heard that very story told by my nursemaid.

In the same way, many witty ideas have been presented at the expense of the honest Meadow-Father <note>original: "Wiesenpater." This likely refers to a specific, perhaps simple-minded or folk-oriented preacher, possibly associated with a pilgrimage site like the "Wies" or simply a "pasture" priest.</note>, and so on.

My dear friend! Your paragraphs are intended to be a sermon about Bavaria's preachers, are they not? But tell me, have you achieved your ultimate purpose? You are supposed to teach, to touch, and to move <note>original: "lehren, rühren, und bewegen." These correspond to the three classical duties of an orator: docere (to instruct), delectare (to delight or touch the emotions), and movere (to move the audience to action).</note>, but you do none of these. The manner in which you write is, in a certain sense, as poorly chosen as the sermons you made fun of; your writing, then, is nothing more than a biting satire, and since you also...

to place there the names of the most honest men, then it <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "but [if you continue] to place there..."</note> becomes a <term>pasquinade</term> <note>original: "Pasquill." A pasquinade is a satirical protest or lampoon, often posted in a public place. In the 18th century, it was a common way to publicly shame enemies.</note>.

My dear Sir! If you had written all the things you recite in your paragraphs with a philanthropic tone, if you had pointed out the faults of the preachers with friendship without publicly shaming your fellow man, if you had convinced us through a noble defense of the truth and carried us away through the power of gentle expressions, then you would have delivered an important work for Bavaria; but as it is, you have missed your ultimate purpose, and the man of feeling, who seeks the truth in a presentation worthy of it, will throw your writing into a corner and say to himself: this man writes pasquinades.

Forgive me my sincerity, my friend! I mean well, and I do not like it when people abroad say of us that we treat each other in an indecent manner; in a country where people hate each other, things will not go well. --- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of his friend's aggressive writing style. The previous page ended with the observation that it is always shameful when a writer forgets civic love and instead chooses a tone that insults others.</meta>

...offends fellow citizens. <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...it always turns out shameful [when one] offends fellow citizens."</note> You, my dear friend! wished to instruct Bavaria's preachers in the doctrine of the Holy Gospel; and you are right to do so: however, the tone with which you do it is so striking, as if you yourself had forgotten the holy teachings of the Gospel, which tells us:

> Love your neighbor as yourself. ——

Or should you perhaps not count these preachers among your fellow men? —— Oh yes, and your own heart will convince you that you have not acted well toward your fellow man. Give me your hand, and promise me to act in the future in a Bavarian and <term>upright</term> <note>original: "biedermännisch." In the 18th century, the "Biedermann" was an idealized figure representing honesty, civic virtue, and middle-class reliability.</note> manner, to combat errors but never to offend people.

The faults of our brethren are always more visible than their virtues, and it is common to mankind to prefer censuring rather than praising. We must extend brotherly hands to one another, not persecute; I shall never in my life forget the saying of a wise man who spoke to his friend:

> —— Well, you have shown me the faults of this man; now show me also his virtues, and then I will judge whether you were not being unjust.

<note>Vocabulary: Bavaria's, Preachers, Gospel, Charity/Love of Neighbor, Fellow men, Bavarian, Upright/Honest, Errors, Virtues, To judge.</note>

<summary>The author concludes his rebuke by urging his friend to balance his criticism of the clergy with Christian charity and civic virtue. He argues that true reform comes from attacking errors and "upright" behavior rather than personal attacks.</summary>
<keywords>Christian charity, Bavaria, religious rhetoric, Enlightenment ethics, Biedermann, fraternal love, polemics, pastoral care</keywords>
